new A Complete Geometric Message-Passing Architecture for Point Clouds

Authors: Nadav Dym, Tal Amir, Gia Chi Dang, Christopher Morris

Abstract: Three-dimensional point clouds are a fundamental data modality, and a large family of machine learning architectures has been developed to respect their natural symmetries to rigid motions and permutations. A principled way to compare these architectures, which vary widely in complexity and cost, is through their expressive power. That is, an architecture is complete if it can distinguish any two inputs not related by the symmetries it respects. Simple distance-based message-passing networks are known to be incomplete, and while there exist complete alternatives, they typically rely on computationally intensive mechanisms like high-order representations of the rotation group or higher-order Weisfeiler-Leman-type tests. In this work, we devise a novel, simple, equivariant message-passing architecture for point clouds, and prove that it is complete. We complement our theoretical result with experiments showing that our architecture separates challenging point clouds that distance-based message passing provably cannot distinguish. Our results suggest that high-order equivariant features may not be necessary to enhance the expressivity of point cloud architectures, and can help settle completeness for other equivariant architectures for which such results are currently unknown. We give initial evidence for this by proving completeness of a slightly modified version of SEGNN.

new Sequential Anytime Valid Tests for Efficient Inference-Time Alignment

Authors: Vishesh Kumar Singh, Sayak Ray Chowdhury

Abstract: Inference-time alignment methods, such as Best-of-N (BoN), improve language model outputs by generating multiple candidates per prompt and selecting among them using a reward model. Much of this cost is wasted: a fixed budget is applied to every prompt, although the computation required by prompts varies widely. Adaptive methods halt once sampled answers appear settled, but their thresholds are tuned heuristics with no statistical control over early-stopping errors. We instead seek an anytime-valid stopping rule that takes an error tolerance $\alpha$ as input and halts only once an answer is certified to hold a majority ($\theta_a > 1/2$) under the generator's distribution. We aggregate candidates by final answer with a reward-weighted self-consistency (RWSC) score and treat stopping as a sequential test over the resulting clusters. Applying Wald's sequential probability ratio test to these clusters yields SPRT-RWSC, which performs well empirically, but its guarantee fails because the leading answer shifts during sampling, and the run is continuously monitored. Our main contribution, e-RWSC, replaces Wald's test with a betting supermartingale (an e-process) for every answer the policy produces, bounding the probability of ever certifying a non-dominant answer by $\alpha$, uniformly over time and across all answers. The RWSC scores drive the bets, so they affect only how quickly a dominant answer $a^\star$ is certified, not validity; certification takes $O(\log(1/\alpha)/d_{\mathrm{KL}}(\theta_{a^\star}\|1/2))$ samples. Experiments across several language models, datasets, and reward models confirm the predicted $1/d_{\mathrm{KL}}$ scaling of cost and show that accuracy improves over baselines at substantially lower token cost.

new FEA solver integration of a composite World Model with a Spiking Neural Network memory Block for computationally efficient modelling of defects and variabilities

Authors: Bassam El Said, Adam Sobey

Abstract: Each fibre-reinforced composite structure has a unique material architecture. The manufacturing methods used and the presence of defects lead to local variations which require material-scale models that can resolve the response in real time at every location in the structure. Concurrent multi-scale schemes provide this capability, but at a prohibitive computational cost for large structures as the number of integration points and time increments scales with size. Surrogates have been studied extensively as replacements for material-scale models to mitigate these computational limitations. A surrogate must represent an arbitrary local architecture including defects, track the irreversible material state over an unknown number of increments, remain stable when driven recursively by its own predictions, and fit within the memory available at every integration point. Here, a Composites World Model based on a Multi-Modal Spiking Transformer architecture is designed to meet these requirements. The proposed architecture combines a material encoder, a Spiking Neural Network memory block that tracks the long-term internal state of the material, and transformer blocks that learn the short-term applied strain state. The system is trained in three stages: material architecture encoding, base training on short time windows, and recursive fine-tuning with scheduled sampling to suppress cumulative error drift. The trained model is disassembled at inference and coupled to a non-linear FE solver, enabling structural scale simulation of composites containing embedded defects. Benchmarks against experimental results for IM7/8552 laminates under tension, compression and transverse shear, for pristine and wrinkled composites, show that the SNN memory block successfully tracks the material internal state and results in prediction errors between 0.4% and 4.8%.

new Learning to Defer under Expert Drift

Authors: Ziyao Mou, Andrea Wynn, Eric Nalisnick

Abstract: The learning to defer (L2D) framework enables safety in machine learning and AI systems by allocating difficult or critical decisions to a human expert. However, most prior work on L2D assumes fixed expert reliability, overlooking temporal fluctuations in human performance due to factors such as fatigue and cognitive biases. Humans commonly exhibit such substantial, systematic performance shifts across a wide range of domains, from high-stakes settings such as radiology, aviation, and judicial decision-making, to everyday tasks such as crowdsourced image annotation. We propose Expert-Drift-Adapted Learning-to-Defer (EDA-L2D), a novel expert-aware L2D framework that explicitly models these temporal shifts by conditioning the deferral head on recent histories of expert outcomes via a sequence model. This history-aware approach enables adaptive deferral policies that anticipate reliability fluctuations and better allocate decisions between human and machine over time. We provide comprehensive experiments across three diverse tasks -- image classification, medical diagnosis, and hate speech detection -- including generalization analyses under realistic deferral conditions and irregular shifts in expert reliability. Our results show that EDA-L2D adapts more effectively than prior L2D methods to diverse temporal patterns of expert reliability, resulting in improved system performance and more effective deferral decisions. Code is available at https://github.com/ZiyaoMou/L2D-Expert-Drift.

URLs: https://github.com/ZiyaoMou/L2D-Expert-Drift.

new Neural Conditional Gaussian Model: Efficient Data Assimilation with Sequential Uncertainty Quantification for Nonlinear Dynamics

Authors: Chuanqi Chen, Nan Chen

Abstract: Data assimilation (DA) in partially observed nonlinear systems requires repeated estimation of both hidden states and their uncertainty. Conventional nonlinear filtering can be computationally expensive and often relies on an explicit dynamical model. In this paper, we develop the Neural Conditional Gaussian Model (Neural CGM), a data-driven dynamical model that maps unobserved physical states into a learned latent space, where the dynamics of the latent states follow a conditional Gaussian structure. This structure allows closed-form DA updates in the latent space, while nonlinear encoder-decoder mappings retain the flexibility needed to represent complex dynamics in physical space. A key feature of Neural CGM is its treatment of uncertainty in DA. The latent posterior mean and covariance are mapped to the physical variables through a Gaussian decoder that combines local moment propagation with learned nonlinear corrections. The resulting physical-space posterior statistics depend on the sequentially updated latent distribution rather than on a separate post-processing estimate. The same statistics enter a Gaussian negative log-likelihood during training, so both the posterior mean and uncertainty affect the learned model. Because Neural CGM learns the dynamical model itself, it can also be used for state forecasting and simulation without explicit knowledge of the governing equations. Numerical experiments on benchmark nonlinear dynamical systems show accurate state estimation and forecasting while substantially reducing online assimilation cost relative to conventional nonlinear DA methods. Neural CGM is therefore suited to repeated state estimation, uncertainty quantification, and prediction from partial observations.

new A new algorithmic approach for finding flat minima: critically damped Polyak stepsizes

Authors: Francesco Caporali, Jason M. Altschuler, Boris Hanin

Abstract: We present a new adaptive stepsize rule that biases gradient descent toward flatter minima in both theory and practice, and establish the first uniform-in-time guarantees for both sharpness and loss reduction for a broad class of locally quadratic objectives. Our critically damped Polyak (CDP) stepsizes multiply the classical Polyak rule by $4$ and a mild damping factor. The factor $4$ is critical in that it produces equioscillations around the manifold of minimizers $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order. Damping provably drives convergence to $\mathcal{M}$. CDP thus operates at the edge of stability by adapting its stepsizes to local curvature. Once the loss is small, its projected iterates follow gradient descent on $\log{\lambda_1(\nabla^2 \mathcal{L})}$ along $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order, paralleling the flatness bias of normalized gradient descent (Arora et al., 2022). Unlike normalized gradient descent, CDP step lengths shrink with the distance to $\mathcal{M}$, enabling convergence to an interpolating solution that locally minimizes sharpness at finite learning rate. We establish our theory for a broad class of non-negative losses with locally quadratic Morse-Bott structure near $\mathcal{M}$, capturing regular interpolation regimes in smooth overparameterized neural networks. Empirically, CDP stepsizes produce a flattening effect similar to that of Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM, Foret et al., 2021), reducing sharpness even in settings beyond our theory, including nonlinear teacher-student networks and fine-tuning of pre-trained LLMs.

new Grid Cells as Spectral Successor Features: A Reusable Basis for Continual Reinforcement Learning

Authors: \'Elodie C\^ot\'e-Gauthier, Doina Precup, Isabeau Pr\'emont-Schwarz

Abstract: Continual reinforcement learning requires agents to adapt when the task changes while reusing what they know about the environment. Successor features separate dynamics from task-specific reward, but their effectiveness depends on the choice of base features. Taking the identification of entorhinal grid cells with eigenvectors of the successor representation as a design principle, we build this basis from eigenvectors of the transition matrix of a fixed reference policy. With such a basis, the successor features at a state-action pair are a linear combination of the base features at that same pair, which is not true in general, and the coefficients are known in closed form: each successor feature is its base feature multiplied by $\theta_i = 1/(1-\gamma\lambda_i)$, where $\lambda_i$ is its eigenvalue. The long-term return is thus predicted from the same features as the immediate reward, no successor-feature network or replay buffer is needed, and adapting to a new task reduces to a linear regression on the reward. The agent acts greedily on the action values of the reference policy, which is one step of policy improvement over it and suffices to reach a single goal. In a two-goal gridworld, with the uniform random walk as reference policy, whose eigenvectors are the low-frequency Laplacian eigenvectors, agents built this way maintain near-maximal return across every change of goal, whereas SimpleSF and DQN lose most of their return at each change.

new Hybrid Retrieval for Government Scheme Search in English and Marathi

Authors: Aishwarya Gunjal, Kanchan Khemnar, Pawan Poojary, Pranav Kulkarni, Raviraj Joshi, Manish Khodaskar

Abstract: Retrieving government schemes requires matching citizens' information needs to formal administrative descriptions, often across languages and terminology. We present a bilingual search system for Maharashtra government schemes and public services that combines BM25 retrieval with sentence-embedding similarity through equal-weight score fusion. The system indexes aligned English-Marathi scheme descriptions and supports multilingual, Marathi-focused, and translation-mediated semantic configurations. Evaluation on manually curated bilingual queries compares BM25, dense retrieval, and hybrid retrieval using IndicSBERT and MahaSBERT. BM25 provides a strong baseline with a mean reciprocal rank (MRR) of 0.864. Hybrid retrieval increases MRR to 0.872 with IndicSBERT and 0.879 with MahaSBERT, whereas the corresponding dense-only systems achieve 0.663 and 0.655. MahaSBERT-based fusion also increases precision from 0.781 to 0.808 while matching BM25 recall at 0.946. These results support the complementary use of lexical and semantic signals in this collection, while showing that improvements over a strong lexical baseline are modest and metric-dependent. The study provides an application-focused analysis of hybrid retrieval for bilingual public-service information access.

new The Window Lever: Longer Observation Windows Outweigh the Observed Effects of Data, Capacity, and Architecture in Customer Behavior Forecasting

Authors: Tara Sowrirajan, Yanqiao Wang

Abstract: In our experiments on long-horizon customer-behavior forecasting, a longer observation window outweighed the observed effects of more data, more capacity and newer architectures. The window is the weeks of history a model sees before the week it forecasts from. Going from an 8- to a 16-week window adds a raw +0.070 to 0.107 log-space $R^2$ in every family at 1M. Matched test cohorts, which score the same customer-weeks at every window, show this raw comparison understates the gain in every family they cover. The direction holds on five public datasets, four spanning over a year (ridge and our transformer on all five). On our internal data, longer windows admit up to 10% more and colder training customers, which matching does not fix. Our design does not separate window length from calendar coverage, because every test anchor (the week a forecast is made from) falls in one eight-week band. On training-period anchors, the weekly ridge probe's gain persists outside the holiday season, keeping about 72% of it. We therefore report a robust positive association with longer windows and do not identify the magnitude of a calendar-independent history effect. Of four internally registered predictions, the only fully blind one failed. An activity-mixture model predicted +0.014 from 24 to 32 weeks; the weekly-aggregate probe returned -0.0004. For the sequence families, the other three levers are about an order of magnitude smaller, bounded and unresolved. Their observed effects are at most 0.006 between the nominal 1M and 10M tiers and 0.0052 among five sequence families at 16 weeks with clocks as built, both single-seed on the full test split, and 0.006 for capacity on three-seed means. These are observed intervals, not equivalence bounds. Our claim is the order-of-magnitude contrast between the window and those bounds. A team building on this class of data should start by lengthening the window.

new Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher

Authors: Yang Meng, Zixin Ding, Robert L. Grossman, Yuxin Chen

Abstract: Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

new Bio-PM-Guided Synthetic IMU Generation

Authors: Deepali Singh, Deepesh Parpani, Aviral Gupta

Abstract: Collecting and labeling inertial measurement unit (IMU) data for human activity recognition (HAR) is expensive, and models trained on one group of subjects often fail to generalize to unseen individuals. Even with balanced data, classifiers struggle to distinguish dynamically similar activity classes and benefit from targeted augmentation. We explore whether synthetic IMU data generated in the representation space of a pretrained biosignal encoder, Bio-PM \cite{b1}, can serve as a practical augmentation source. Rather than synthesizing raw signals, we leverage Bio-PM's frozen encoder to convert 10-second IMU windows into token embedding sequences and train class-conditional decoders in this space: (1) an autoregressive transformer decoder that predicts tokens sequentially, and (2) a diffusion decoder that learns the token distribution through a denoising objective. We analyze generation realism through distributional metrics and embedding-space visualizations, evaluate downstream utility through leave-one-subject-out HAR classification, and present a PatchDecoder for approximate raw waveform reconstruction. Our diffusion decoder achieves a Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real (TSTR) macro-F1 of 0.68, demonstrating that generated tokens are realistic enough to train a classifier that transfers to real data. Combining synthetic-token augmentation with a 320-dimensional gravity-aware feature representation raises LOSO macro-F1 from 0.729 to 0.809 over a real-only baseline across 51 subjects on the WISDM benchmark, with statistically significant gains concentrated in the most challenging activity classes. These results demonstrate that high-fidelity generation in a pretrained encoder's representation space is achievable and practically useful for cross-subject HAR augmentation.

cross LLM Complexity: Towards a Computational Complexity View of Large Language Model Inference

Authors: Yu-Hung Wu, Shao-Xiang Yuan, Chan-Hung Yu, Zih-Ching Chen, Kuan-Hao Huang, Shao-Hua Sun

Abstract: Computational complexity theory offers a principled framework for characterizing the efficiency of computational problems, and Large Language Models (LLMs) can similarly be viewed as computational systems executing a structured process of input, inference-time compute, and output. In this context, the number of generated tokens emerges as a natural and directly observable proxy for inference-time compute. However, we show that the tempting analogy between LLM token length and classical time complexity does not hold. We disprove the hypothesis that inference-time token usage aligns monotonically with classical time complexity, demonstrating a fundamental divergence grounded in how LLMs actually allocate computation during inference. To this end, we introduce LLM Complexity, a new metric designed to quantify problem complexity from the perspective of LLMs by characterizing the area bounded above by the accuracy-token curve. We find that diverse LLMs induce highly consistent task rankings under LLM Complexity, indicating that the metric captures stable notions of difficulty across models. We leverage this property to construct an easy-to-hard curriculum for supervised fine-tuning, which improves accuracy while reducing token consumption. Finally, we demonstrate that LLM Complexity generalizes to real-world tasks, producing more stable cross-model correlations than human-annotated difficulty labels.

cross ICARE: cross-domain text classification with incremental class-aware representation and distillation learning

Authors: Son Duong, Truong X. Tran, Hai-Anh Tran

Abstract: The rise of powerful, fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) necessitates robust natural language inference (NLI) systems. These NLI systems ideally train across various tasks, encompassing diverse concepts like sentiment analysis, news classification, and coreference resolution. However, this continual, multi-tasking approach can lead to catastrophic forgetting, where previously learned tasks are lost, especially in domains lacking specific labels. Rehearsing past examples with labels can further exacerbate this issue by causing overfitting, hindering the system's ability to classify new categories. To tackle these challenges, this research proposes ICARE, a novel framework with two key components: an instance selection module and a knowledge distillation method. The former is based on representation learning, which meticulously selects training examples to present to the NLI system. The latter, knowledge distillation with feature and logit-level transfer, facilitates knowledge transfer between tasks. This leverages pruning techniques and optimized objective functions to transfer both essential feature-level and decision-making (logit-level) knowledge, mitigating catastrophic forgetting while adapting to new patterns. Extensive evaluations on benchmark text classification datasets have demonstrated ICARE's effectiveness. The framework achieves a significant improvement in target accuracy and reduction in the ``forgetting metric''. These results solidify ICARE's ability to preserve and leverage learned knowledge across diverse tasks, ultimately enhancing the adaptability and performance of NLI systems. The source code is available at https://github.com/CongSon01/ICARE_NLP.git

URLs: https://github.com/CongSon01/ICARE_NLP.git

cross When Is Program Structure Required? Certificate-Grounded Calibration of Program-Induction Difficulty

Authors: Wenzhang Du

Abstract: A program-induction benchmark is informative when its examples require the structure it aims to test. Yet a two-rule generator can produce demonstrations that a single rule also explains. We introduce certificate-grounded calibration, which constructs matched tasks with minimum solution lengths of one and two in a bounded string-replacement language. Complete one-rule enumeration and an executable two-rule witness certify the change before model inference. We evaluate 120 reference families with three to six shared examples, using 1,440 new model trajectories and 480 saved trajectories. Across Qwen3-8B and Olmo-3-7B-Think, displayed-task completion is 23.53-45.83 percentage points lower on certified separation tasks, while success on a wholly unshown witness pair is 8.40-13.45 points higher. These witness gains accompany lower agreement on uniformly sampled strings and 1.93-5.78% agreement on inputs that activate both reference rules. Recertification also reveals that a single unchanged example can exclude a shortcut and determine whether two rules are required. Together, these results show how the evidence supplied by examples shapes both the required solution and the programs models produce. The construction provides an executable testbed for comparing program search, final-answer delivery, and behavior on new inputs under verified task requirements.

cross Improving On-board Shape Models Using Sun Sensors and Implicit Representations

Authors: Zacharia A. Rudge, Giacomo Acciarini, Dario Izzo, Dominik Dold, Moritz Fieback, Said Hamdioui

Abstract: We propose an original, end-to-end approach to generate and refine the onboard 3D shape model of irregular small bodies, such as asteroids, using only low-cost Sun sensor eclipse data and coarse a priori knowledge, tailored for resource-constrained missions like CubeSats. cross DL101 The Representation Capability of Neural Network Models

Authors: Fernando Berzal

Abstract: Universal approximation theorems (Cybenko, Funahashi, Hornik & Leshno) provide the mathematical justification for why neural networks are powerful enough to model complex, nonlinear phenomena. Shallow networks can approximate any continuous function on compact sets. Leshno's theorem clarified that non-polynomial activations are necessary for universality, which explains why ReLU, logistic, hyperbolic tangent, and other common activations work (but pure polynomials do not). However, approximation theorems provide no efficiency, training, nor generalization guarantees. They do not indicate how many neurons are needed for a given accuracy, whether gradient descent will find the right parameters, or whether networks trained on finite data will generalize well. Barron provided quantitative error bounds for particular function classes (approximation rates for functions with bounded Fourier norm), whereas Mont\'ufar was the first to show that expressivity grows exponentially with depth (in terms of the number of linear regions). Deep networks are strictly more expressive than shallow ones with the same number of parameters. Depth is fundamentally more powerful than width, providing a rigorous mathematical explanation for the use of deep learning models.

cross Risk-optimal active learning with informative abstention

Authors: Fariborz Setoudehtazangi, Geoffrey J. McLachlan

Abstract: We study label acquisition when an oracle may abstain and its response probability shares parameters with the classifier. A query can then provide information through both a returned label and the response outcome. Under a correctly specified parametric model, we derive the classifier information after adjustment for response-model nuisance parameters. Combining this information with the local curvature of multiclass zero-one risk gives an acquisition criterion for minimizing the leading asymptotic excess risk. We characterize the optimal allocation under a fixed query budget and show how query allocation affects response identifiability. The oracle criterion reduces to response-discounted acquisition when response probability does not depend on the classifier parameters. Simulations examine the resulting query allocations and isolate the contribution of the response-outcome term through an ablation. A Consumer Complaints replay retains observed human abstentions and reveals benchmark labels upon response. Response-aware acquisition has lower mean test error than response-discounted acquisition at the two largest budgets, with paired differences significant after Holm correction. Entropy sampling performs better at the smallest budget. The gains depend on the response mechanism and query budget.

cross Variance-Adaptive Upper Confidence Bounds for Multi-Armed Bandits with Kaplan--Meier Survival Estimation

Authors: Yangfan Cui, Andrew Papanicolaou

Abstract: We study the stochastic multi-armed bandit problem in which the reward associated with each arm is the survival probability at a pre-specified time horizon, and the observed outcome is a right-censored time-to-event variable. Standard bandit algorithms face delayed and partial observations in this setting. Prior adaptations for the Kaplan--Meier (KM) estimator rely on Hoeffding-type bounds that ignore the inherent heteroscedasticity of continuous survival processes. To bridge this gap, we propose KM-UCB, a variance-adaptive policy that unites continuous-time survival analysis with discrete sequential decision-making. By leveraging martingale theory and continuous influence functions, we derive a fixed-cohort empirical Bernstein bound parameterized by Greenwood's variance formula. For realtime allocation, we retain the Greenwood index and use a survival bound from completed records to review the recommended arm. Under catch-up reporting, the policy has logarithmic expected regret with an arm-specific variance term in the exploration bound. We evaluate the original policy in a dual-layer delayed simulation and a retrospective replay of breast cancer trial data, and evaluate the correction in a separate paired simulation.

cross Infer3D: Inverse Generative Modeling Enables Robust 3D Reconstruction

Abstract: Humans can understand the 3D structure of scenes even from highly unfamiliar or extreme viewpoints. Cognitive science suggests this generalization stems from a top-down generative inference strategy. In contrast, state-of-the-art 3D reconstruction neural networks typically rely on amortized, feedforward inference. While fast, this pattern-matching approach degrades when faced with out-of-distribution (OOD) scene conditions unseen during training. cross Empirical Variational Bayes Factorization for Longitudinal Matrix Data

Authors: Jiakun Li, Eric F. Lock

Abstract: Collecting high-dimensional data over time for multiple individuals is increasingly common in biomedical research, particularly in studies of disease progression or molecular time-course experiments. Often data are missing or the timepoints are irregularly spaced across individuals. cross PriTabICL: Differentially Private Tabular In-Context Learning with Task-Aligned Cluster-Wise Synthesis

Authors: Syed Ahzaz Shah, Evan Bramblett, H\'eber H. Arcolezi, Chen Gong, Qianqian Tong, Xiaochen Li

Abstract: Tabular in-context learning (TabICL) enables large language models (LLMs) to predict target records using a handful of labeled demonstrations in their prompts. However, these demonstrations often contain sensitive personal information. Differential privacy (DP) offers principled protection, but noise-based methods introduce substantial noise, distorting the feature-label relationships essential for accurate prediction, especially as data dimensionality grows. In this work, we study DP data synthesis for generating privacy-preserving TabICL demonstrations. We identify two limitations of directly applying general-purpose DP synthesis to entire datasets: modeling a global distribution can obscure local structure and minority patterns in heterogeneous data, while measuring broad attribute dependencies under a limited privacy budget can reduce the accuracy available for relationships most relevant to prediction. More broadly, global statistical fidelity may fail to preserve heterogeneous structures needed by downstream predictive and generative models. To address these challenges, we propose PriTabICL, a general framework compatible with existing DP synthesis algorithms. It partitions records into DP clusters and synthesizes each independently, using a fixed feature-target workload to prioritize prediction-relevant statistical relationships. At inference, each query is routed to a DP cluster, and demonstrations are sampled from its synthetic dataset without additional privacy loss for the original records. Experiments on four real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our methods across heterogeneous datasets. In particular, one of our instantiations, PriTabICL-PrivSyn, improves over global PrivSyn in 8 out of 12 settings and outperforms existing private TabICL approaches in nearly all evaluated settings. Open-source code is available.

Abstract: Enabling AI systems to autonomously improve AI models requires agents capable of performing machine learning engineering (MLE). cross CAIMS: Scalable Cross-Array In-Memory Search

Authors: Yi-Chun Liao, Liu Liu, Ruiyang Qin, Yiran Chen, X. Sharon Hu

Abstract: Search, especially top-k similarity search, plays a vital role in many applications, but its performance can be severely limited by memory access overhead. In-memory search (IMS), an alternative to CPU/GPU-based approaches, exploits massive parallelism within a content-addressable memory (CAM) array to perform search in situ and provides both low latency and high energy efficiency. However, the capacity of a single CAM array is often insufficient for modern workloads, requiring data to be distributed across multiple arrays. Deploying scalable top-k similarity search across multiple CAM arrays poses a fundamental problem that existing designs fail to address: how to accurately and efficiently reduce local binary match results to global top-k results. This work introduces CAIMS, a cross-array IMS framework that is agnostic to memory technologies and applications, enabling scalable and efficient top-k similarity search while preserving accuracy. To enable accurate top-k result reduction, CAIMS leverages the existing threshold match functionality with a combined offline and online parameter selection approach to transform local binary match results that lack globally comparable similarity information into global coarse-grained similarity scores. To further improve efficiency, CAIMS conducts hierarchical reduction and transfers only the selection results and necessary control signals with lightweight local peripherals. Implemented on a 3D NAND Flash model and demonstrated using the retrieval phase of retrieval-augmented generation, CAIMS improves throughput by up to $22.3\times/10.1\times$ and energy efficiency by $426.6\times/23.7\times$ compared to the server-class CPU baseline and prior 3D NAND Flash accelerators, respectively, while maintaining comparable accuracy.

cross Blind Spots Travel, Knowledge Stays Home: What RL Inherits from an LLM Judge

Authors: Liang Yue, Eric A. Vance

Abstract: LLM judges serve as rewards where no answer key exists, in the hope that their knowledge passes to the trained model. In 814 RL runs that teach 7-8B models to answer closed-book questions or abstain, we ask what passes. What a grader knows about single answers stays home. A wrong answer that a judge rewards is memorised for that one question. A model trained with the answer key, or with a far stronger judge, chooses which new questions to answer no better than the untrained model does by sampling 32 answers and abstaining where they disagree; the grader's knowledge adds about one corrected answer per hundred questions. What a grader believes about a kind of question travels. A judge that rejects eleven of the twelve correct training answers about capitals of former states teaches the model to refuse 98% of such capitals on new questions, a loss that accuracy and abstention rates do not reveal. Changing only those twelve verdicts by hand moves the refusal from 5% to 90%, even for a grader that accepts as many answers as the answer key; at that leniency, two of four judges already cost 38-42% of these capitals. The capitals are not forgotten: with "I don't know" blocked, the model answers 94% of them correctly. The grader therefore needs no knowledge of its own: rewarding the model for giving the answer that most of its own untrained samples agree on, with no labels and no judge, earns the answer key's reward and keeps the category.

cross Stability Bands for Feature Attribution: The Core, the Nucleolus and the Bargaining Set of Explained-Variance Games

Authors: Fernando Delbianco, Fernando Tohm\'e

Abstract: Shapley-value attributions are the default way of explaining machine-learning predictions in economics, but they report a single number per feature and say nothing about whether a coalition of features that jointly accounts for more than it receives could contest that allocation. We study feature attribution through the core, the nucleolus and the bargaining set of an explained-variance game, in which the worth of a set of features is the variance of the model's predictions that those features generate. For every feature we report a stability band (its range of shares over the core, or over the least core when the core is empty), with the nucleolus as distinguished point and a certificate that the allocation admits no justified objection. Three results organise the approach. First, for linear models the game is exactly 2-additive, with pairwise Harsanyi dividends $2\beta_i\beta_j\sigma_{ij}$, so multicollinearity is what creates coalitional structure. Second, in every 2-additive game the prenucleolus coincides with the Shapley value, the (least) core is point-symmetric around it, and the core is non-empty if and only if every cut of the interaction graph has non-negative weight. The Shapley value thus always lies in the prebargaining set, and stability adds information only through band widths and interactions of order three or more. Third, for convex games the bands have a closed form and any allocation can be certified with one minimum cut. In a hedonic price model, the usual mean-SHAP explanation admits a justified objection by the coalition {Ratings, ROM, Battery}. In a gradient-boosting forecaster of retail orders the exact game has an empty core and strong higher-order interactions; the nucleolus departs from the Shapley value by up to 0.19 variance shares, and sparse third-order surrogates recover it where pairwise ones cannot.

cross When Objectives and Evaluators Disagree: Evaluating Transfer for Post-hoc Demographic Framing Mitigation

Authors: Tunazzina Islam

Abstract: Post-hoc rewriting is used to reduce demographic disparities in LLM-generated text when the source model is inaccessible. A rewrite can look successful merely because it improved its own target or satisfied its selector. We study this as a transfer problem on 660 gender pairs and 1,980 age contrasts of demographic-conditioned climate messages. A rewriter proposes edits, a selection model chooses among constraint-satisfying candidates, and an independent evaluator uninvolved in either rates pairwise framing disparity. A fixed policy that always targets the message with the lower Persuasion Bias Index (PBI) transfers inconsistently across axes and outcome aggregations. An adaptive policy that proposes edits on both sides but accepts at most one significantly improves on that rule, applied to the same candidates, in seven of eight comparisons across two evaluator families, mostly by editing more pairs, not by guided direction choice, at higher preservation risk. A policy editing both messages gives the largest point reductions on fewer pairs. These two policies reduce disparity across a preservation-threshold sweep; absolute ratings vary across evaluator families and prompts. Shrinking the PBI gap increases dimension-mean disparity under both evaluator families. Mitigation should be reported as transfer across objectives, evaluators and their prompts, and editing policies.

cross LLMs Are Already Confidence-Aware Zero-Shot Classifiers

Authors: Owen Cook, Xingyi Song

Abstract: Zero-shot large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as text classifiers, but their labels are only useful when we know to what extent we can trust them. We compare black-box methods (verbalised confidence and sampling) with white-box methods that read token probabilities (P(True), and multiple logit-scoring methods using constrained generation). We evaluate eleven open-weight LLMs (1B-32B parameters) on five classification datasets with 3 to 151 labels, measuring classification performance (accuracy, F1-macro), discrimination (AUROC), and calibration (ECE and Brier score). The choice of confidence method rarely impacts accuracy significantly, but logit-based scoring methods lead to much better discrimination (AUROC 0.74-0.77) than black-box methods (AUROC 0.57-0.66). Replacing labels with single-token identifiers preserves discrimination but costs 3-6 accuracy points on average. We also introduce decision-only scoring, which only scores positions where labels diverge in the prefix tree (trie). It reduces decode steps by 88-96% on the three largest label sets and performs comparably to full-label scoring. The best open-weight model-method configurations (Gemma 4 31B and Qwen 3.5 9B) are competitive with the commercial Jev 1.13.0 classifier.

cross VoxForge: A Unified Foundation Model for Dense 3D Features Across MRI, CT and PET

Authors: Rushil Anirudh, Jayaraman J. Thiagarajan

Abstract: A unified 3D medical foundation model promises reusable, frozen features that serve a wide variety of tasks such as segmentation, classification and registration on MRI, CT and PET as well as specialist models do. Generalist models still need separate backbones for localized and global tasks, specialist models are confined to a single modality, with the concern that spanning modalities under-emphasizes fine anatomy, and both typically rely on end-to-end fine-tuning to be competitive. We present VoxForge, a single encoder that integrates recent advances in 3D architecture, self-distillation and cross-modal curricula to learn transferable features from permissively licensed MRI and CT volumes. We evaluate it strictly frozen, the test a reusable foundation model must pass, against state-of-the-art generalist and specialist models on segmentation, classification and registration across MRI and CT, and on PET, which it never sees. VoxForge has the best or tied-best average rank in every task group, serves localized and global tasks across modalities at the level of state-of-the-art specialists, and is extremely label-efficient, matching top-performing models on TotalSegmentator CT with a tenth of the labels.

cross When Should Deployed BCI Decoders Adapt? A Resource-Priced Ladder for Frozen EEG Trunks

Authors: Sergey Musienko

Abstract: When should a deployed MI decoder spend adaptation resources-not only how to adapt under shift? We study a thin control layer outside a frozen encoder: a resource ladder (factory -> Bayesian head -> affine map, with full fine-tune as an escape hatch) governed by regime-aware gating under a utility that prices accuracy, fit time, and label cost. The head is Bayesian in the conjugate sense: a Normal-Inverse-Wishart prior over the class conditional Gaussians in embedding space, updated in closed form (no gradients) as labelled trials arrive. Full estimators, constants, and the reanalysis code are in the companion paper and the ancillary bundle of this submission. Thesis: adaptation should be gated and capped, not always-on and not blindly fine-tuned. Evidence: (i) utility-max gating over-adapts on clean data that already carries session drift; a clean-referenced gate restores low false-entry rates (e.g. dropping the adaptation rate from 0.56 to 0.11 on field-like sessions, with the cascade staying on the factory decoder for ~87% of clean cells). (ii) Under severe synthetic stress, a lightweight LDA head matches or exceeds the frozen trunk's accuracy across five encoder architectures (n=155 subject x trunk cells, pooled across shared subjects), at ~23x lower fit time than full warm-start fine-tuning. Always-on baselines favor capping the default below full fine-tune. (iii) In two no-perturbation BNCI session checks the factory model transferred without intervention (no reliable head gain), so the control layer's value under natural drift is cost-efficiency and clean-safety rather than demonstrated accuracy gains. (iv) Adaptation severity does not follow a universal ordinal ladder across datasets, highlighting the need for dynamic gating over fixed adaptation policies.

cross RE-CAM: Readout-Enhanced Content-Addressable Memory for Quantitative Similarity Search

Authors: Yi-Chun Liao, Dongyoung Lee, Jiazheng Chen, Tergel Molom-Ochir, Tomohisa Kawakami, Tania Roy, Ik-Joon Chang, X. Sharon Hu, Hai "Helen" Li, Yiran Chen

Abstract: Similarity search is essential to many applications but incurs massive data movement when executed on conventional architectures. Content-addressable memory (CAM) is a promising hardware substrate, as it allows similarity search directly in the CAM array, minimizing data movement and offering both low latency and high energy efficiency. However, conventional CAM array readout produces only binary match results, discarding quantitative similarity information that could be further exploited. Extracting this information requires interpreting the CAM array output voltage that evolves during a CAM search, making the sensing timestamp a crucial design variable alongside the reference voltage and decoding parameter. In this work, we propose RE-CAM, a readout enhancement framework for CAM that is agnostic to memory technology and application, enabling CAM array readout to approximate linear and nonlinear functions with one CAM search. By reusing the comparators to sense the time-varying output voltage multiple times, jointly optimizing the three design variables under system constraints and hardware nonidealities, and co-designing the optimization algorithm and reconfigurable hardware, RE-CAM achieves accurate approximation with low energy and area overhead without introducing a readout throughput bottleneck. Across linear, piecewise-linear, sigmoid, and exponential functions, RE-CAM reduces normalized root mean square error by 10.89% to 24.34% for general approximation and 29.43% to 46.73% when exploiting dataset distribution, compared to a shared uniform-reference ADC baseline with the same comparison budget. Relative to the same baseline, RE-CAM also achieves $8\times$ higher search throughput while reducing energy per search and area by 47% and 39%, respectively.

cross Fundamentals for Binary Classification Under Label Noise

Authors: Dimitrios I. Diochnos

Abstract: This chapter surveys fundamental results for binary classification under label noise. Binary classification under label noise involves training models on datasets where class labels are partially incorrect, leading to degraded performance and misclassification. We distinguish poisoning attacks from noise processes and formalize random classification noise, Massart noise, and malicious classification noise within the probably approximately correct (PAC) framework. These models capture different adversarial strengths and data-generation realities. We then highlight some core ideas and representative results. In particular, we explain why random classification noise preserves the expected ordering of hypotheses and thus supports learning using the empirical risk minimization (ERM) principle, how sample complexity depends on the noise rate, and why learning algorithms often require an upper bound on the corruption level, as well as how such a bound may be estimated from data in the case of random classification noise. We also discuss computational aspects of ERM under noise and present a canonical negative result technique -- the method of induced distributions -- within the malicious noise model where the adversary has more power and may affect not only the label of an example but also the instance. We conclude with pointers to related work and a taxonomy that organizes classical label-noise models using the NCAR/NAR/NNAR lens.

cross Causal Discovery with False Positive Error Control

Authors: Erik Jahn, Venkat Chandrasekaran, Frederick Eberhardt, Leonard Schulman

Abstract: Causal discovery methods produce data-driven hypotheses about causal relationships, which may become new scientific findings or inform decisions about downstream interventions. In such settings, false positive causal claims can be more consequential than false negatives. Yet few methods target false positive error control in causal discovery, and existing error guarantees are often asymptotic and require strong versions of the faithfulness assumption. We introduce the $m$-conservative PC algorithm and prove that it controls false discovery rate in the linear Gaussian setting under an extremely weak version of the faithfulness assumption. In our framework, false positive error for causal graphs is defined based on the conditional-independence information they entail, yielding a metric that penalizes both false adjacencies and incorrect edge orientations. The parameter $m$ quantifies a trade-off between algorithmic complexity and the strength of the required faithfulness assumption. The $m$-conservative PC algorithm combines undirected graph learning, a Benjamini-Yekutieli-style procedure for conditional independence testing, and a conservative orientation step that marks unresolved or contradictory edge directions as ambiguous. Our algorithm produces valid outputs and retains error guarantees even in the finite-sample setting, despite possible random errors in the conditional independence tests.

cross Instance-Optimal Moment Estimation in the Add-Remove Model of Differential Privacy

Authors: Shokichi Takakura

Abstract: In this paper, we study the problem of estimating empirical means, variances, and covariances under pure differential privacy in the add-remove model. While existing mechanisms achieve optimal worst-case error, they may not be optimal for individual datasets. To measure the performance of mechanisms on individual datasets, we introduce instance-dependent error metrics and prove sharp lower bounds on them. Then, we design mechanisms that achieve these lower bounds and demonstrate that they achieve asymptotically better utility compared to existing methods.

cross ActMem: From Latent Memory Reasoning to Grounded Robotic Action

Authors: Linfeng Dong, Jianwei Niu, Jiuyi Zhao, Ruibing Feng, Xinghao Wu, Wu Yang, Shuai Tao, Wulong Liu, Mu Li

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown promising results in real-world long-horizon manipulation, where memory plays a crucial role. However, existing memory-augmented VLAs either retrieve past observations without reasoning about what they imply, or reason over the full history in a shared context where it crowds out current perception. Cognitive science treats them as distinct systems: memory reconstructs past experience to decide what to do, while perception guides how to act. We therefore propose ActMem, an in-context memory framework that decouples memory reasoning from perceptual grounding within a single vision-language backbone. Built on a vision-language model with native video understanding, ActMem infers the current subtask from a timestamped interaction history and keeps the hidden states formed during this inference as a Latent Memory Chain-of-Thought (Memory CoT), which conditions a flow-matching action expert. Perception Attention further lets the expert attend directly to the current view. To keep inference fast under long histories, Efficient Memory CoT Inference reuses computation across consecutive decisions without changing the output. ActMem achieves 71.1% task success and 80.8% completion success on RoboMemArena, surpassing the strongest baseline by 7.5 and 8.7 percentage points, and 73.8% average success on MIKASA-Robo, the best among all compared methods. In the real world, it reaches 68.8% average success on exogenous, endogenous, and traceless memory tasks, 10.0 percentage points above the strongest memory-augmented VLA. Code and checkpoints will be released at https://github.com/LigphiDonk/ActMem and https://huggingface.co/donk111/ActMem.

URLs: https://github.com/LigphiDonk/ActMem, https://huggingface.co/donk111/ActMem.

cross A four-level executable validation protocol for evaluating LLM-generated topology specifications of physics-based HVAC digital twins

Authors: Younghwan Joo, Jeonghoon Han, Sanghyun Oh, Soosik Bang, Sung-il Kim

Abstract: Physics-based digital twins of air conditioning, a principal energy consumer in manufacturing and buildings, can be generated automatically from a declarative specification of its equipment, connections, and sizing. Large language models (LLMs) now write such specifications from plain-language descriptions. Evaluations of these specifications stop at parse success, completed runs, or textual similarity, and cannot reject a model that converges in simulation but is physically wrong. In this study, a four-level protocol is proposed that tests whether an LLM-written specification not only executes but also yields a physically valid digital twin. The protocol was applied to eight LLMs and fourteen HVAC topologies, five describing an existing low-humidity test chamber. To test for authorship bias, engineers and a second model family also described six topologies. Pooled pass rates varied by under five percentage points across description authors. These results imply that the physics hardest to model in each field, here the desiccant rotor, must enter the generation framework as validation levels. cross Joint Language Modeling for In-the-Wild Speech-Scene Generation with Distinct Token Representations

Authors: Semin Kim, Ju Yeon Kang, Sunghwan Ahn, Seonuk Kim, Nam Soo Kim

Abstract: Speech-scene generation aims to synthesize intelligible target speech together with diverse environmental sounds as a coherent acoustic scene. The task is considered challenging in two respects. Speech and environmental audio differ substantially in their semantic and acoustic characteristics, yet existing approaches typically model the complete scene using a single mixed acoustic representation. At the same time, the two sounds are closely related in real-world speech scenes, whereas conventional approaches usually rely on simulated scenes mixed from independently recorded sources. We propose a large language model (LLM)-based framework for joint modeling of distinct speech and environment token streams. A source-aware neural audio codec extracts temporally aligned speech and environment tokens from mixed audio, and a pretrained LLM generates both token streams in an interleaved fashion conditioned on the natural-language scene description and speech content. This formulation preserves source-specific representations while modeling the token streams within a shared generative context. Since both streams are extracted directly from mixed recordings, the model can also be adapted to in-the-wild speech scenes without isolated sources. Our experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework improves speech intelligibility, scene relevance, and audio quality over existing speech-scene generation techniques, with consistent gains from real-world adaptation. We further investigate the effects of distinct token representations and interleaved generation, and analyze whether the environmental sound remains stable while speech is active and how the two streams interact.

cross When and why can integral constraints improve the accuracy of physics-informed neural networks?

Authors: Myeong-Su Lee

Abstract: Adding constraints obtained by integrating governing equations to the loss has been reported to improve the accuracy of physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) in several applications, but mathematical understanding of these benefits remains limited. For several partial differential equations (PDEs), we derive stability estimates for losses with and without integral constraints. The comparison reveals that in certain parameter regimes, the standard loss has very weak control over specific error components, and that the integral constraints restore uniform control of these components. This explains when and why the constraints improve accuracy. Finally, numerical experiments support this interpretation. In parameter regimes where the standard loss is predicted to have weak control over those components, the two losses indeed show clear differences in accuracy. Conversely, in regimes where the standard loss retains sufficient control, both losses yield similar accuracy.

cross Learning Generative Ranking Policies for Whole-Page Optimization

Authors: Chen Qiu, Zhigang Hua, Qi Xu, Jizhe Zhang, Sha Hua, Ren Mao, Luke Simon

Abstract: A recommendation page is consumed as a whole: the items on it compete for attention and complement one another, so the best page is not simply the individually best items placed side by side. We study whole-page optimization, which selects and orders the items on a page jointly to maximize the score of the page. The number of possible pages grows combinatorially, so good pages must be generated rather than enumerated. Some generative re-ranking methods use the same page-level training signal for every choice in a sampled page. Others imitate target pages built before training, which remain largely fixed as the generator improves. We address both limitations with one local page-level objective, which repeatedly moves the current generator toward pages with higher score. This objective splits exactly into one training target per position, and each target compares candidates at the same partial page by the scores of the complete pages they lead to. Every position therefore learns to choose the item that is better for the whole page, rather than the item that looks best on its own. We call this procedure whole-page policy improvement. On two public datasets, KuaiRand-1K and RL4RS-SeqSlate, which cover multi-objective item selection and constrained ordering, and on an industrial dataset of logged pages, our method outperforms the evaluated generative re-ranking baselines. On a synthetic benchmark with a known optimal page, it also comes closest to the optimum, showing that its improvements come from better whole-page decisions.

cross Differentially Private Score Estimation with Applications to Score-Based Generative Models

Authors: Mengxiao Gao, Elly K. H. Hung, Yuchen Wu, Yi Yu

Abstract: Estimating the score function of a distribution plays a central role in statistics and machine learning, with applications in distribution estimation, sampling, and score-based generative modeling. However, learning scores from sensitive data raises privacy concerns. These concerns are particularly pronounced in score-based generative modeling, where trained models may memorize individual training examples and expose sensitive information through membership inference and data extraction attacks. Although non-private score estimation has been extensively studied, the statistical limits of its private counterpart remain less understood. In this work, we study score estimation under $(\alpha,\beta)$-differential privacy for $d$-dimensional sub-Gaussian distributions with locally $\gamma$-H\"older-continuous scores. We develop a sampling-based private score estimator using quadratic B-splines and the exponential mechanism, and we implement our proposal through projected Langevin Monte Carlo with runtime polynomial in the sample size $n$. The proposed estimator satisfies $(\alpha,\beta)$-differential privacy, and achieves expected density-weighted squared error $\mbox{polylog}(n) \max(n^{-2\gamma/(d+2\gamma+2)},\,(n\alpha)^{-2\gamma/(d+\gamma+1)})$. We also establish a nearly matching private minimax lower bound, thereby characterizing the privacy-accuracy trade-off for score estimation. As an application, we construct a differentially private diffusion model whose privacy guarantee is independent of the number of denoising steps. We show that, with an appropriate discretization scheme, the resulting diffusion model attains nearly minimax optimal expected total variation error among all private distribution estimators. We validate our theoretical findings through experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets.

cross Dynamics of Protected State Evolution

Authors: Qinyou Wang

Abstract: States with identical protected observations can admit different future interventions. Each transition specifies its successor, response increment, required initial reserve and net consumption. Composing these records in execution order distinguishes protocols with equal net consumption and different initial reserve requirements. At a fixed state, a weighted Gram operator determines the linear response ellipsoid and a quotient identifies directions attainable only through cooperation between blocks. Nonlinear systems with identical complete affine data can have incomparable finite-budget response sets. In regular charts, the first correction to quadratic minimum cost is a covariant cubic form involving the third derivative of cost and the Hessians of protection and response. At singular gates, sequential feasibility depends on the peak reserve along the path. A history-weighted operator detects responses outside the old channel's range closure. cross Physical Laws without Physics Loss? Probing Learned Dynamics in Video World Models through Forced Response

Authors: Yutao Li, Chen Wang, Wangmeng Zuo, Hui Li

Abstract: Do video world models learn physical laws without a physics loss, or merely imitate motion? Overall motion and appearance scores cannot settle it. We investigate this through nonlinear forced response: an excitation shakes the support of a suspended object. We introduce dual visual-numeric conditioning and adapt six image-to-video backbones to predict this motion using only the flow-matching loss. Wan predicts motion accurately and generalizes to unseen excitations, objects, poses, and scenes. Counterfactual tests suggest capabilities beyond replaying training videos. But what laws have they learned? We design three tiers of tests using Taylor expansions of the dynamics and geometry. All six reproduce the linear response, including resonance; five capture higher-order dynamics, including nonlinear softening. Remarkably, softening is barely present in training: only 0.74% of the swing's squared displacement. Extensions to a double pendulum and a rocking block show sustained dynamics are learned more faithfully than brief impacts. Yet learned dynamics may fail to appear when state estimation or propagation goes wrong. We turn these tests and findings into strategies to improve video training and generation.

cross What One Sample Cannot See: Why Fewer Denoising Steps Can Score Higher on Physics Benchmarks

Authors: Yutao Li, Chen Wang

Abstract: When a storm can head north or south, a forecast down the middle may score better under squared error than either plausible path. The same paradox appears when video generators are tested as world models: fewer denoising steps can raise physics scores even as videos blur or stop moving early. Why? Physics-IQ compares each generated video with one recording, although a scene can have multiple valid futures. An exact decomposition shows its scores can reward reduced variation among generated futures, even when their distribution becomes less accurate. We introduce Kernel Physics-IQ to remove this incentive in expectation, needing only two independent samples per condition. Two-step sampling lowers Kernel Physics-IQ in all five image-to-video models. For CogVideoX, the official score rises by 0.055 while ours falls by 0.131. Changing the sampling schedule reveals further differences official scores miss. In controlled physical systems with known outcome probabilities, our metric favors the true distribution over collapsed predictions. In short, a world model should be judged not only by how closely one video matches what happened, but by how faithfully its predictions represent what could have happened.

cross Erasing the Wrong Thing: Conditional Attribute Leakage and Concept Erasure in Shared Foundation-Model Embeddings

Authors: Haijun He, Fei Li, Donghong Ji, Junjun He

Abstract: Medical-image embeddings are increasingly shared in place of raw images for authorized downstream tasks, yet they may reveal sensitive attributes. Standard concept-erasure methods target population-level guardedness. cross AdsNet: A Graph Neural Network for Direct Adsorption Energy Prediction

Authors: Changzhi Ai, Frank Abild-Pedersen, Johannes Voss

Abstract: Fast and accurate prediction of adsorption energies is critical for high-throughput computational catalyst screening, existing methods compromise either on evaluation speed (full-graph machine learning potentials) or geometric sensitivity (coordination-based models like the alpha-scheme). Here, we present AdsNet, an equivariant graph neural network that directly predicts adsorption energies in a single forward pass, which operates on a local graph restricted only to adsorbate atoms and its immediate surface neighbors within a cutoff radius. This local graph formulation decouples computational complexity from total system size while encoding continuous geometric features (interatomic distances and directional vectors) enabling sensitivity to subtle variations in local bonding environments. AdsNet adopts a message-passing architecture with scalar and vector node features over multiple interaction layers. Trained on AdsTraj, a dataset of 33,920 density functional theory (DFT) calculated structures spanning 26 transition metals and 6 adsorbates (H*, C*, O*, CO*, OH*, HOCO*), the model achieves an adsorption energy root-mean-square error (RMSE) of 0.032 eV and a force RMSE of 0.047 eV/A. Analytic forces obtained via automatic differentiation enable structure relaxation through a projected gradient descent algorithm with adaptive line search, eliminating entire geometry optimizations. Additionally, AdsNet uniquely decomposes predicted adsorption energies into contributions from individual atoms within the local adsorbate graph - an interpretability feature unattainable with conventional machine-learning potentials. AdsNet thus establishes an accurate, highly efficient, and interpretable framework for large-scale adsorption energy prediction and local interface relaxation across various material surfaces.

Abstract: Deep learning models that predict drug--target interactions (DTI) can speed up drug discovery, but the candidate drugs they score are valuable intellectual property that their owners cannot reveal. cross From Geographic Memory to Spatial Generalization in Large Language Models

Authors: Chen Chu, Zhaonan Wang, Cyrus Shahabi, Hengcai Zhang, Feng Lu

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can associate places with geographic descriptions, but recovering an observed association does not establish an ability to generalize across geographic space. We investigate whether continuous location representations improve this generalization and encourage geographic knowledge to become spatially organized within an LLM. Layer-wise probing and representation clustering provide additional evidence consistent with spatially organized geographic representations. Together, these findings show the value of evaluating spatial generalization separately from memory and of incorporating continuous spatial representations into language models.

cross Safeguarded Learning-Assisted Nonlinear Conjugate Gradient Methods

Authors: Morteza Kimiaei, Saman Babaie--Kafaki

Abstract: A learning-assisted framework for Nonlinear Conjugate Gradient (NCG) methods is developed in which lightweight online prediction is used to support, rather than replace, the classical optimization process. The proposed approach retains the standard two-term NCG structure and introduces two independent online extreme learning machine models. The first model predicts an NCG coefficient during restart recovery or when persistent stagnation is detected, whereas the second model is used only after a line-search failure in finite precision arithmetic to propose a bounded correction to the deterministic fallback step size. Neural predictions are accepted only when they satisfy deterministic numerical safeguards, including descent, scale, and out-of-distribution requirements; otherwise, the corresponding deterministic recovery or fallback mechanism is retained. Both learning models are updated online using quantities already generated along the optimization trajectory and therefore require no additional function or gradient evaluations for training. These learning frameworks are applied to six well-known classical NCG methods, producing corresponding low-storage learning-assisted variants with deterministic safeguards. Numerical experiments on 480 CUTEst test problems show improved robustness for all six variants at broadly comparable evaluation costs. A PDE-constrained conductivity-identification example further illustrates the potential of the proposed approach to reduce evaluation costs and computational time for problems with expensive function and gradient evaluations. These findings suggest that restricted online learning can improve the robustness and computational performance of classical NCG methods while preserving their low-storage structure and deterministic optimization safeguards, and is particularly promising when function or gradient evaluations are expensive.

cross CGM: Chaos-Guided Graph Attention and Expert Routing for Autism Stereotypy Detection

Authors: Hafsa Aslam, Yue Li, Saba Aslam, Abdur Rasool, Qiao Pan, Muhammad Rizwan, Ovidiu Bagdasar

Abstract: Skeleton graph attention for autism video analysis weights joints by content alone, giving an erratic wrist the same standing as a still one. Stereotypy, however, is defined by how unpredictably movement unfolds rather than how much of it occurs: in our cohort, every amplitude-based kinematic descriptor falls in stereotyped clips, while windowed Sample Entropy of angular velocity alone rises (Hedges' g = +1.05). We compute this irregularity as a per-joint, per-frame tensor that structurally drives computation. The proposed chaos-guided graph attention and mixture-of-experts model (CGM) couples two mechanisms. First, a learned pairwise term added to the graph-attention logit concentrates attention on irregular joints and re-ranks the remaining edge weight toward jointly irregular pairs; its unconstrained coefficient neither vanishes nor inverts, and zeroing the tensor realigns attention in 94% of clips. Second, a gate conditioned on the same signal routes each frame to two of four experts, whose weights track frame-level irregularity. Under subject-grouped five-fold cross-validation on 116 clips, CGM reaches 0.930 balanced accuracy, exceeding the graph-attention baseline by +0.187; the component ordering replicates on the public MMASD dataset, and on held-out Panoptic Studio sessions, CGM keeps 0.86 specificity without retraining, against at most 0.35 for chaos-free baselines. In ablation, routing without irregularity yields no reliable gain, whereas chaos modulation alone contributes +0.132. A severity index from the same tensor reaches 0.87 split-half reliability, separates the cohorts with Hedges' g = +1.08, and stays consistent within children across therapy recordings, supporting video-only monitoring of stereotypy during intervention.

cross Can LLMs Resolve Entity Conflicts in Domain Specific NLU Pipelines?

Authors: Akansha Bhardwaj, Mingbo Cui, Victor Guimaraes, Dominik Kohler, Diana Nicoleta Popa, Meghana M. Reddy, Paolo Rosso

Abstract: Resolving entity conflicts is a critical step in Pipeline-based NLU architectures, especially when multiple extractors provide overlapping or conflicting outputs. Traditional rule-based entity conflict resolution methods often lack flexibility and are unscalable. In this work, we aim to study if the broad knowledge of LLMs can be leveraged to resolve entity conflicts in domain specific NLU pipelines. We present an experimental comparison between a traditional rule-based conflict resolution method, and two LLM-based approaches: a multi-shot prompting method using the Llama3.1-8B-Instruct model and a LoRA fine-tuned Llama3.1-8B-Instruct model. Results show that while multi-shot prompting offers ease of adaptation and rapid behavioral changes, the fine-tuned model delivers superior performance on complex, ambiguous cases. Ultimately, the best approach appears to be a combination of both strategies, leveraging multi-shot prompting's flexibility alongside the broad accuracy gains of LoRA fine-tuning for a more comprehensive performance.

cross Beyond Error Mitigation: Using Boosting for Noisy Quantum Machine Learning

Authors: Anastasiia Lukianenko, Yash J. Patel, Nurbolat Kenbayev, Vedran Dunjko

Abstract: Quantum error mitigation (QEM) can recover information about ideal quantum computations from noisy circuit executions, but may require a large sampling overhead. Fundamental lower bounds show that recovering noiseless observables without problem-specific information can require superpolynomially many samples, with exponential overhead for broad classes of noisy circuits. However, problem-specific information can reduce the number of samples needed to obtain the required answer. We investigate this possibility in supervised quantum machine learning, where labeled examples provide information about the function to be learned. Binary classification provides such a setting, since correct prediction depends on the sign and margin of the classifier output rather than on accurate reconstruction of every noiseless expectation value. We develop a theoretical and empirical framework for comparing boosting of noisy parameterized quantum circuits with probabilistic error cancellation (PEC), requiring both methods to reach the same learning target. For the specified circuit architectures under Pauli noise, we establish a common hypothesis-class complexity bound: matching empirical margin loss gives the same upper bound on expected classification risk. We derive resource criteria and evaluate AdaBoostR and Real AdaBoost in finite-shot teacher-student simulations across circuit depth and physical noise strength. Under the same classification-accuracy target, boosting requires fewer circuit executions as depth and noise increase. Boosting also achieves higher test accuracy than the corresponding reference. For the supervised-learning problems studied, these results demonstrate a task-dependent resource advantage: exploiting information retained in noisy outputs can avoid the cost of ideal-output reconstruction without sacrificing observed classification performance.

cross Closing Performance Gap for Diffusion Language Models under Low Inference Budgets

Authors: Hao Wu, Sipeng Chen, Jingwen Fu, Yuwang Wang

Abstract: Diffusion language models offer an architectural route to low-budget generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, which can reduce the number of sequential decoding steps relative to token-by-token autoregressive decoding. However, realizing this advantage at low budgets is challenging, as generation quality often declines when the number of denoising and refinement steps is reduced. Editable diffusion models address this quality loss through post-draft refinement, but useful intermediate results may still be lost before final submission. We introduce CaRR (Commitment-aware Refinement and Recovery), a training-free framework which improves submission quality from a fixed editable checkpoint through updated-context refill and trajectory-based recovery within its existing forward schedule. Token Commitment Remasking (TCR) remasks selected native pure replacements and refills them on the next scheduled edit forward using the updated context. Submission Commitment Recovery (SCR) uses the resulting refinement trajectory to recover supported candidates when the final response does not satisfy task-specific output requirements. Both modules operate within the native mask/edit schedule without additional backbone forwards. Experiments on code generation and mathematical reasoning show consistent gains in average accuracy over native ME-DLM across four inference budgets, with the largest gains under tight budgets. At one-eighth of the full scheduled forward budget, CaRR improves the six-metric average accuracy by 7.44 pp, including gains of 11.37 and 10.05 on MBPP and MBPP+.

cross Anchor and Check: Deterministic Cross-Prompt, Cross-Model Reconciliation for Auditable LLM Decisions

Authors: Sivakameswara Reddy Chejarla

Abstract: Pipelines that let a large language model (LLM) make discrete decisions need to know which decisions they may act on unattended, and a record that explains each one. On 48,000 model-item evaluations over 6,000 questions, with every method given access to verbalized confidence as a tie-breaker, the main finding concerns where calls come from: pooling another family's samples raises semantic entropy's AUROC by 1.7 to 4.0 points at every budget, and a same-model temperature check is the weakest design. As detectors of the anchor's errors, the contract is ahead of pooled entropy with 3 and 5 samples under both tie-breaker conventions; with 10 samples the point estimates differ by less than one point and the sign depends on the convention. Under the strict Agree gate it automates 70% of items and still acts on 16% of errors. What the contract adds is a fixed committed answer, an explicit outcome and a decision replayable from its record; generation itself was not reproducible on the hosted API.

cross Limits of local curvature for constructing search subspaces in segmentation models

Authors: Maxim Bolshim (ITMO University, Saint Petersburg, Russia), Alexander Kugaevskikh (ITMO University, Saint Petersburg, Russia)

Abstract: We examine how well subspaces constructed from local curvature represent the change in the gradient update field after a finite step in semantic segmentation models. The local response is the action of the curvature operator on the actual input displacement. In a study of 50 images and two models, we measure the ratio of the loss increase attained in a fixed subspace to that attained in the full space. This ratio decreases as the search radius increases. On ten images from the same sample, we compare the one-dimensional subspace spanned by the initial gradient with two rank-16 subspaces for both models. The two equal-rank subspaces are assessed at the same initial states, using the same actual steps and measured vectors. Adding generalised Gauss-Newton directions to the initial gradient reduces the mean relative error in representing the field change from 0.922 to 0.874 for the first model and from 0.819 to 0.763 for the second. At the same rank, including the response direction for the initial gradient nearly eliminates the error in representing the local response. The mean error in representing the field change does not decrease. A decomposition with fixed pixel weights shows that the finite-step remainder is comparable in norm to the field change and makes the largest diagonal contribution to the squared projection error of the field after the step. Evaluation of a search subspace should measure how accurately it represents the field change and the local response.

cross Rank2Replace: VLM-Ranked Slot Replacement for Out-of-Distribution Generalization

Authors: Bo-Han Feng, Keng-Te Liao, Shou-De Lin

Abstract: Spurious correlations can give image predictors high in-distribution accuracy but poor generalization under distribution shift.
