cross Full Rank and Stable Sampling of $\tanh$ Networks

Authors: Philipp Grohs, Mat\v{e}j Tr\"odler

Abstract: We study the recovery of feedforward $\tanh$ networks of fixed architecture with $P$ parameters from finitely many point evaluations. First, we characterize the set of parameters where the differential of the realization map has full rank and show that it coincides with the regular parameters in the sense of Vla{\v{c}}i{\'c} and B{\"o}lcskei (2021). Second, we make use of this characterization to prove that almost every tuple of $2P+1$ sampling points yields uniform-norm Lipschitz stability on compact subsets of this parameter set. Third, we prove that for exact observations, projected gradient descent and projected Gauss--Newton converge locally from sufficiently close feasible initial guesses. The same evaluations also determine the realized function for arbitrary parameters and identify parameters in the characterized set up to permutations and sign changes of hidden neurons. Finally, we develop a general framework for real-analytic networks that highlights the role of full rank in stability and local reconstruction.

cross One Schedule Does Not Fit All: Adaptive Inference Schedules for Diffusion Language Models

Authors: Rabimba Karanjai, Linsey Pang, Hemanth Hegadehalli Madhavarao, Richard Williamson, Uma Kona, Prakhar Mehrotra

Abstract: Diffusion language models typically use a fixed denoising schedule across workloads. We show that the cheapest schedule that preserves quality depends on the task, model, and sampler. We propose Task-Calibrated Denoising Allocation (TCDA), a deployment rule with an explicit error guarantee. Given candidate samplers, paired calibration data, a utility tolerance, and guardrails against degeneration, TCDA uses simultaneous one-sided bootstrap bounds to certify which candidates meet both the utility and guardrail criteria. It deploys the cheapest certified candidate or falls back to the full schedule. The probability of deploying an infeasible schedule is asymptotically at most alpha. Candidates include early prefix decoding, state-reusing continuation, reduced time grids, and confidence-threshold decoding. Across seven datasets and two model families, TCDA certifies reductions in denoiser evaluations by factors of 3.4 on XSum and 2.0 on WMT14 for ELF-B; 8.0 on WMT14 and 4.1 on GSM8K for LLaDA-8B; and 1.33 on OpenWebText for both ELF-M and ELF-L. A validation protocol fixed before generation confirmed every selection on fresh seeds. Across 500 calibration splits, false certification remained below 2% in every setting at a nominal level of 5%. Certified schedules vary across tasks and model scales. Trajectory measurements suggest that when decoded tokens commit is more informative for schedule selection than the remaining latent discretization error. We also find that the ELF reference sampler draws its time grid at random, so reproducible schedule comparisons for unconditional generation require fixing the grid.

cross The Price of an Explanation: What Demanding Verifiable Artifacts Costs, and What It Buys Back

Authors: Edgar A. Du\'e\~nez-Guzm\'an, Eduardo Due\~nez, Edgar Said Hernandez Sanchez, Suzanne Sadedin

Abstract: Generative models produce many candidate solutions but provide no intrinsic signal by which to rank them. One mitigation is to demand a \emph{verifiable artifact}: a program that can be executed against the available evidence. A common concern is that requiring executable verification incurs costs in both solution quality and computational budget. We measure these two costs on the $120$-task ARC-AGI-2 evaluation set. Demanding a verifiable artifact from an LLM reduced the score from $93$ to $68$ of $120$ tasks ($77\%$ to $57\%$). Of those $25$ tasks, $21$ were reached by no program-producing configuration we ran, at any budget, by sampling or by search; yet for $27$ of the $28$ tasks that only the answer-only arm solved, some program arm held a program that reproduced every demonstration and still failed the test. The bottleneck is obtaining an explanation that \emph{generalizes}, not finding one. A short evolutionary search over two already-sampled programs, at no additional model cost, beats best-of-$2$ sampled code by $9.2 \pm 1.4$ tasks, and over a single sampled program beats $1$-shot sampling by $9.9 \pm 1.3$. Moreover, because a program can be checked against the training examples, any program-producing protocol can terminate early once a task is explained: under the same stopping rule, best-of-$n$ sampling declines up to $38\%$ of its budget and the evolved arm up to $49\%$, whereas an answer-only baseline cannot terminate early. Applying the same methodology to decoder synthesis for quantum error correction yields interpretable decoders evaluated against a certified panel of published decoders on held-out exam shots.

cross Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding

Authors: Youssef ElNahas, Adel Ammar, Soumaya Chaffar, Wadii Boulila

Abstract: Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.

cross Hellinger Decision Trees for Density Ratio Estimation: Finite-Sample Guarantees

Authors: Ren-Rui Liu, Zheng-Chu Guo

Abstract: Density ratios play a central role in two-sample comparison, distribution-shift adaptation, and generative-model evaluation. However, estimating a density ratio through separate estimation of the underlying densities can be unnecessarily challenging. In this paper, we develop a direct approach to the log density ratio estimation by minimizing a balanced Hellinger risk over additive ensembles of axis-aligned decision trees, trained using Newton boosting. We establish finite-sample guarantees for the estimator produced by the algorithm and derive an explicit convergence rate. Under suitable tail and H\"{o}lder smoothness conditions, together with an additional assumption on the progress made at each boosting iteration, our rate matches, up to logarithmic factors, the classical minimax-optimal $ \mathscr{L}^{2} $ rate for function estimation. A key technical contribution is an optimization analysis that exploits the special exponential structure of the Hellinger risk. In particular, we show that the decrease in the second-order Taylor approximation (which is computable) of the empirical risk controls the actual decrease in that risk, yielding a direct optimization guarantee for the boosting algorithm. This allows us to establish statistical convergence guarantees for the algorithm's output without relying on an idealized empirical-risk minimizer. Experiments on synthetic data corroborate the predicted convergence behavior, while experiments on public datasets demonstrate the practical effectiveness of the proposed estimator.

cross The Depth of a Prediction: Where Language Models Resolve a Word Predicts Human Reading Time

Authors: Donato Riccio, Fabrizio Maturo

Abstract: Surprisal from a language model's final layer predicts how long people read a word, yet the model computes its prediction across many layers. We read each word's prediction at every layer with the tuned lens and summarize this layerwise surprisal trajectory by its resolution depth, the depth around which its change is centered. On eight English eye-tracking and self-paced reading corpora (81,957 words) and ten GPT-2, OPT and Pythia models, words that a model resolves later, and the words after them, are read more slowly than equally surprising words. Pooled over corpora, the effect holds with corrected word probabilities and beyond first-to-last-layer divergences, and a whole-trajectory functional regression adds no significant information beyond resolution depth.

Abstract: The Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA) will observe the millihertz gravitational-wave sky, including millions of long-lived Galactic binaries (GBs), tens of thousands of which are expected to be individually resolvable throughout the mission's lifetime. Abstract: The lack of built-in security in controller area networks (CANs) exposes modern vehicles and industrial systems to masquerade attacks that can compromise safety and reliability. Results: Our approach significantly reduces NRMSE by 30% compared to TV. End-to-end training improves SWS estimation over separate image reconstruction and SWS calculation. Conclusion: Accurate SWS quantification is possible at acceleration factors up to 19. Our method significantly outperforms TV, highlighting the need for data-driven regularization in this challenging MR problem. Further, our approach successfully generalizes to in vivo data. Significance: We present the first end-to-end trainable MRE reconstruction method for estimating SWS maps directly from k-space. NN-based reconstruction can enable rapid stiffness mapping for dynamic studies, functional imaging, and real-time clinical feedback.

cross Reward misalignment as an opportunity for materials discovery

Authors: Ryotaro Okabe, Shoki Kishida, Abhijatmedhi Chotrattanapituk, Dominic Bollettieri, Takuya Isogawa, Yongqiang Cheng, Mingda Li, Masaki Adachi

Abstract: Reward misalignment is usually regarded as a failure of artificial intelligence. In materials discovery with generative AI models, however, disagreement between predefined rewards and expert judgment may reveal materials that the rewards overlook. We investigate this possibility using electronic flat bands as a prime example. Experts mostly agree with a numerical bandwidth score, but their disagreements are systematic and cannot be explained by an estimated 5% rate of mistaken labels. We learn the experts' preferences from pairwise comparisons and use them to guide a crystal diffusion model. The learned preferences and numerical score agree on the flattest bands but diverge elsewhere. For example, the preferences favor KCo2As2 and CoGa, reported compounds with partially flat bands that the score would discard. The framework also extends to synthesizability and phonon properties. Our findings suggest that reward misalignment can expose opportunities for materials discovery beyond what fixed rewards are designed to find.

cross Backbone Matters: Benchmarking Encoder-Decoder Networks for Semantic Segmentation of Driving Scenes on BDD100K

Authors: Ehsan Rassekh

Abstract: Semantic segmentation gives an autonomous vehicle a dense, pixel-level description of its surroundings and is a core part of scene understanding. Most segmentation networks consist of an encoder (the backbone) and a decoder, yet the backbone is often chosen without a systematic comparison. This work studies this choice on the BDD100K driving dataset. Four widely used decoders (U-Net, FPN, LinkNet and PSPNet) are combined with 13 backbones from six families (ResNet, VGG, DenseNet, Inception, MobileNet and EfficientNet), and all 52 models are trained with one common protocol. The best model, an FPN decoder with an EfficientNet-B4 backbone, reaches 62.6% mean intersection over union (mIoU), 89.8% accuracy and a loss of 0.092. Only two of the 52 models, both FPN with EfficientNet, exceed the 56.9% mIoU reported with the BDD100K benchmark. The effect of the backbone depends strongly on the decoder: the same EfficientNet-B4 gives 42.6% mIoU with PSPNet but 62.6% with FPN, and the gap between the best and the worst backbone ranges from 12.0 points (U-Net) to 42.5 points (FPN). The results and the scope of this study are discussed.

cross Physics-Informed Feature Representations and Pretrained Tabular Foundation Models for Generalizable Melt Pool Geometry Prediction

Authors: Rafid Enayet (University of South Carolina), Imtiaz Ahmed (West Virginia University), Md Habibor Rahman (University of South Carolina)

Abstract: Developing generalizable, data-efficient machine learning (ML) models for advanced manufacturing remains challenging because experimental datasets are limited and process-property relationships vary across materials and operating conditions. Tabular foundation models generally achieve stronger classification performance than classical baselines, particularly for multi-material datasets. cross TwinLab-Bench: Evaluating AI Scientists in Agentic Twins of Real Labs

Authors: Yang Wan, Linchao Zhu

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly expected to act as AI scientists, which requires three increasingly open abilities: carrying out a given computational workflow correctly, choosing the next measurements from a given design space, and designing their own experiments. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate the first two abilities, in a terminal or in a closed loop that selects from predefined candidates. Evaluating the third ability requires reliably executing an experiment that an agent designs, with readings grounded in a real laboratory. We introduce TwinLab-Bench, which evaluates AI scientists on self-designed experiments in 52 tasks from laboratories in the physical sciences, the life sciences, and engineering. The benchmark supports more than 100 experimental action types, and the agent's report is scored by comparison with a hidden answer taken from real laboratory records. Each task has its own TwinLab agent, an agent that serves as the environment and executes the experiments that the tested agent designs, reading the raw materials of the laboratory and calling calculators that are built from its records. Compared with a language model that sees only the task description, the TwinLab agent almost eliminates readings that deviate from the real records and inconsistent answers to a repeated request, and closed-book probes control for answers recalled from memory. We find that these tasks remain hard for frontier models: the strongest, Claude Fable 5.2, averages a score of only 45.0, and we conduct an empirical study of where it fails.

cross A Statistical Framework for Training Data Auditing of Large Language Models

Authors: Shengjie Niu, Yeheng Ge, Jian Huang

Abstract: Training-data auditing asks whether data of interest were used to train a large language model (LLM), a question central to privacy auditing, copyright protection, and benchmark-contamination detection. These concerns are particularly relevant to the use of copyrighted books and scientific papers in pre-training, as well as synthetic text and medical question-answer records in fine-tuning. In practice, training membership signals may be concentrated in only a few passages of a long document, making it important to aggregate evidence within each document without diluting these localized signals. Beyond individual documents, auditing a target collection requires an overall assessment based on the document-level evidence. In this paper, we propose a unified statistical framework for document-level membership inference, target-collection global auditing, and suspicious-subset identification. We integrate complementary chunk-level membership evidence with Cauchy combination to construct document scores for document-level membership inference. The document scores and their calibrated p-values further enable an overall assessment of training-data collection use and the identification of suspicious documents through multiple testing. Under the stated exchangeability conditions, we establish finite-sample validity of document-level p-values, type-I error control for global auditing, and false discovery rate control for subset identification. Case studies on BookTection, Pile-arXiv, Cosmopedia, and HealthCareMagic demonstrate the effectiveness and reliability of the proposed framework in auditing pre-training and fine-tuning data.

cross Who Moved My $Z$ Boson? Revealing Resonance Features Inside a Neural Network

Authors: Kevin Alberto Urqu\'ia Calder\'on, Midori Kato, Inar Timiryasov, Oleg Ruchayskiy

Abstract: Methods used in mechanistic interpretability have revealed human-aligned concepts inside language and image models. cross Scalable fault vulnerability analysis for ReRAM-based process-in-memory CNN accelerators

Authors: Aniseh Dorostkar, Hamed Farbeh, Hamid R. Zarandi, Meysam Sharifzadeh

Abstract: Resistive random-access memory (ReRAM)-based processing-in-memory (PIM) accelerators offer a highly efficient platform for parallel matrix-vector multiplications (VMM) in neural networks, leveraging analog computation, high density, low leakage, and non-volatility. However, their susceptibility to permanent faults, such as stuck-at-high (\textit{SaH}) and stuck-at-low (\textit{SaL}) resistance states, permanently corrupts the convolutional neural networks (CNNs) weights mapped onto ReRAM cell conductance values, thereby degrading inference accuracy and leading to system unreliability in safety-critical applications. Fault injection is a commonly used technique to assess the vulnerability of neural networks by systematically injecting faults into individual parameters and measuring the resulting accuracy degradation. Although this method is accurate, it is prohibitively time-consuming and computationally expensive, especially for large-scale CNNs. In this paper, we propose a fast, scalable, and analytical framework to quantify the fault vulnerability of convolutional neural networks deployed on ReRAM-based PIM accelerators. This method quantifies vulnerability at different granularities, including weights, kernels, and layers, without the need for exhaustive simulation, enabling the design of fault-tolerant accelerators for edge and safety-critical applications. The comparison between the results of the proposed vulnerability score (VS) metrics with performance metric of CNNs captured with fault injection simulations confirms the effectiveness of our proposed method. Unlike exhaustive fault injection, which requires days or weeks, whereas the proposed approach achieves a speedup of about $10^4\times-10^5\times$, enabling fast and accurate vulnerability analysis for large-scale CNNs within only a few minutes or hours.

cross Conformal Selection for Hierarchical Streaming Data

Authors: Zhenyuan Sun, Chengyao Yu, Ruixing Ming, Bingyi Jing

Abstract: Many real-world tasks involve making sequential decisions about whether to select candidates before their responses of interest are revealed. In this work, we address the online selection problem in the context of hierarchical data, where candidates arrive sequentially from different populations. This extends existing online conformal selection frameworks beyond the standard i.i.d./exchangeable data assumption. The primary challenge lies in constructing valid statistics for each candidate sequentially under the hierarchical data structure. To this end, we first develop Online Hierarchical Selection (OHS), a distribution-free method that enables principled candidate selection for hierarchical data streams. Specifically, we introduce methods to construct rank-based e-values for both between-group and within-group samples, and apply averaged e-values to the online e-BH procedure. We then develop OHS with feedback (OHSF), which boosts the power of OHS by leveraging potential response feedback. Theoretically, the proposed methods achieve false discovery rate control under mild hierarchical exchangeability and data arrival assumptions at any time. cross Perfumer: A Large Language Model-based Agentic Framework Accelerating Structural Relaxation and Electronic Band Prediction for First-Principle Calculation

cross Simulation-pretrained autoencoders versus PCA for compressing multimode fibre transmission matrices

Authors: Callum J. Wilson, Terry Wright, Qing Yang, Zhong Wen, Michael P. Pound, George S. D. Gordon

Abstract: Multimode optical fibres can image through a thin, flexible probe once their transmission matrix (TM) has been measured, but a TM is large and must be re-measured whenever the fibre moves. A compact model of feasible TMs would make storing and re-estimating them cheaper, so we ask how compactly TMs can be represented, and from how few measured TMs. Here we benchmark a permutation-equivariant autoencoder, pretrained on simulated fibres and adapted to measured TMs, against principal component analysis (PCA). At 62x compression, adapting the pretrained autoencoder with just two measured TMs reconstructs the TM of a 50um fibre well enough to focus light at 98% of the ideal enhancement, and it stays accurate when the TMs are shifted by 25 pixels or zoomed by 10%, which quickly degrades a linear basis. It is not, however, a better compressor: PCA is about ten times more accurate when fitted on all 163 real TMs (1.1% against 11.9% error on held-out TMs) and is already more accurate from ten. The autoencoder's value is as a prior when few or no real TMs exist: with none it reaches 29% error, against 91% for PCA fitted on the same simulation. Transfer to a different fibre is only partial. The most important extension, not yet tested, is to recover pixel-basis TMs from fewer measurements than a full TM, which would turn the pretrained prior from a compressor into a measurement-saving tool.

cross Flow Matching from Non-Gaussian Sources: A Theory of Efficient Sampling

Authors: Yuchen Zhou, Lili Zheng, Yuxin Chen, Gen Li

Abstract: Flow matching is a versatile framework for generative modeling that transports a source distribution to a target data distribution. A key advantage is its flexibility in the choice of source distribution, such as heavy-tailed and multimodal sources. Yet finite-step sampling guarantees for non-Gaussian sources remain limited. We establish a nonasymptotic convergence theory for Euler discretization of flow matching with linear interpolation. Suppose the source distribution on $\mathbb{R}^d$ has an everywhere positive, continuously differentiable density, finite total variance $\mathsf{TotVar}_{\mathsf{base}}$, and an $L$-Lipschitz score. Using the population velocity field and a suitable time grid, this sampler achieves total variation error at most $\varepsilon$ in $\widetilde{O}(Ld\mathsf{TotVar}_{\mathsf{base}}/\varepsilon)$ iterations, provided the target distribution satisfies a mild second-moment condition. These assumptions cover, among many examples, source distributions such as multivariate Student's $t$ distributions with more than two degrees of freedom and finite Gaussian mixtures with shared covariance, while accommodating target distributions with neither a smooth density nor bounded support.

cross Statistical Inference with Ill-Conditioned Random Weight Neural Network Sieves

Authors: Giovanni Ballarin, Lyudmila Grigoryeva, Lukas Gonon

Abstract: Random weight neural networks (RWNNs) are shallow networks whose hidden-layer parameters are randomly generated and then kept fixed, while only the output coefficients are estimated. Conditional on the sampled parameters, RWNN regression is a linear sieve problem with random approximation space. We show that such random sieves cannot remain uniformly well-conditioned as their dimension grows. For parametrized feature families satisfying polynomial covering and Lipschitz conditions, the eigenvalues of the population covariance matrix are proven to have polynomial upper bounds determined by the covering geometry of the parameter space, leading to a vanishing smallest eigenvalue. We develop approximation, estimation, and inference theory that explicitly treats the ill-conditioning. Under a $\beta$-H\"older source condition, we obtain both faster-than-Monte-Carlo $L^2$ approximation rates and new $L^\infty$ approximation bounds based on stratified sampling. We then derive improved consistency guarantees for ridge-regularized least squares driven by ridge effective dimension, spectral, and leverage-based stability conditions. For inference, we establish asymptotic linearity and Gaussian limits for linear functional directions while allowing the smallest eigenvalue of the population covariance matrix to vanish. Nondegeneracy is directional: we characterize it with a ridge-normalized Rayleigh quotient and extend it to smooth functionals via Riesz representation. Consistency of ridge-adjusted variance estimators is proved, yielding feasible studentized inference. Numerical experiments illustrate the deterioration of random-feature conditioning and the finite-sample behavior of the resulting estimation and inference procedures.

cross How to Effectively Manage the Energy in Residential Systems: Development and Comparison of Methods

Authors: Julian Ruddick

Abstract: The transition from fossil fuels presents significant challenges in energy management. To ensure a sustainable energy future, it is crucial to reduce energy consumption when possible and to synchronize electricity consumption and storage with the production from sources with low greenhouse gas emissions. This thesis explores a variety of energy management methods used to manage batteries, heat pumps, and other energy-related systems. The thesis presents three simulation cases alongside one real-world experiment to compare energy management done through simple rules, optimization, and machine learning methods. Furthermore, this thesis introduces TreeC, a novel machine learning-based method that learns understandable yet efficient energy management strategies, applicable to many cases. The three key contributions of this thesis can be summarized as follows: 1) The developed TreeC method demonstrates competitive performance compared to a variety of energy management approaches, such as model predictive control and reinforcement learning, across a diverse set of energy management problems. The understandability of TreeC's energy management strategies greatly improves the transparency and trustworthiness of machine learning-based energy management. 2) The real-world experiment highlights the need to validate energy management methods on actual hardware and not solely on simulations. Both TreeC and model predictive control performed worse than in simulations, due to, respectively, insufficiently representative training data and the real-time input data not being reliable enough. 3) Energy management algorithms reduce costs in electrified Belgian dwellings, particularly in poorly insulated dwellings, electric vehicle chargers, and photovoltaic installations. However, on average, these cost savings do not yet outweigh the current costs and effort required to implement such algorithms.

cross Exact Bayesian layers via characteristic functions

Authors: William H. W. Thompson, Guillaume St-Onge, Laurent H\'ebert-Dufresne, Jean-Gabriel Young

Abstract: Bayesian neural networks provide principled uncertainty estimates, but training them with Monte Carlo sampling can be expensive. Sampling-free variational inference algorithms make training Bayesian neural networks feasible, but existing approaches assume a Gaussian output distribution, which can be inaccurate in applied problems. Here, we introduce CF-Prop, an uncertainty propagation method that uses characteristic functions to compute the exact output distribution of a Bayesian ReLU layer, without any sampling, even in non-Gaussian settings. Because CF-Prop propagates characteristic functions, the evidence lower bound---the objective of variational training---can be computed in closed form for a broad class of likelihoods, including the Gaussian, Poisson, and Gamma. This allows to implement an efficient resulting variational inference algorithm, CF-VI. On CIFAR-100, CF-VI achieves the lowest calibration error of any tested method, improving on the next best method by about 35%. On a heavy-tailed insurance claim dataset, CF-VI accurately captures tail uncertainty that is severely underestimated by both other sampling-free methods and Monte Carlo methods.

cross Should Belgians Install an Advanced Energy Management System in Their Electrified Dwelling ?

Authors: Julian Ruddick, Cedric De Cauwer, Thierry Coosemans, Maarten Messagie

Abstract: As dwellings electrify and more complex electricity pricing spreads in Belgium, the question arises: What are the potential benefits of installing an advanced energy management systems in an electrified Belgian dwelling ? To answer this question, we simulate 500 representative Belgian dwellings in which fossil systems are replaced by controllable electric technologies: space heating via air-to-air heat pumps, domestic hot water via electric boilers, and private transport via electric vehicles with home chargers. Photovoltaic systems are assigned according to current adoption rates. We evaluate two advanced energy management systems (EMS): model predictive control (MPC) and TreeC, a machine learning method using decision trees to model the EMS strategy. The data and code to reproduce this study are available at https://github.com/EVERGi/should_belgians_install_advanced_ems_paper.

cross Scaling classical hardware for accurate real-time quantum error correction

Authors: Chenyang Jin, Susanne F. Yelin, Andi Gu

Abstract: Fault-tolerant quantum computation requires a classical decoder fast enough to process syndrome measurements at the rate at which they are generated, and to return its decisions with low latency. This is most demanding for superconducting processors, whose fast syndrome cycles force a trade-off between decoding speed and accuracy. Decoders fast enough to keep up sacrifice accuracy, which must be compensated for with larger codes and thus additional physical qubits. Here we introduce Rapids, a streaming neural decoder that is both fast and accurate. We achieve this by co-designing the decoder with a field-programmable gate array (FPGA): the network is built from operations that map cheaply onto the device and trained in its integer arithmetic. For the distance-19 surface code, Rapids decodes each round of syndromes in under a microsecond on a single FPGA, fast enough for superconducting processors, and returns its decisions with the low latency that fault-tolerant algorithms require. Its logical error rate is 240 times lower than that of existing real-time hardware decoders. The same design extends to the color code and the [[144,12,12]] bivariate-bicycle code, on which our decoder is 16 times more accurate than the real-time Relay decoder. We further show that the decoder scales: spread over several FPGAs or hardened into custom silicon, it becomes faster, more accurate, or able to decode larger codes. Classical hardware thus becomes a further resource, alongside qubits, that can be scaled to improve fault-tolerant quantum computers.

cross Bayesian optimization on amortized search space

Authors: Tristan Fauvel, Federico Pavone, Titouan Guillou, Pau Redon Mu\~noz, Chlo\'e Geoffroy

Abstract: In real-world black-box optimization problems, a mechanistic simulator is often available that approximately predicts the behavior of the system as a function of the parameters being optimized. Here, we show how an amortized inverse map of such a simulator can be used in Bayesian optimization to restrict the search space while using a Gaussian process kernel that is aware of the original problem geometry. This allows for efficiently leveraging the low intrinsic dimensionality of the optimization problem. We characterize its approximation bias and information gain, and derive an intrinsic-dimension-dependent regret rate under additional geometric and Gaussian Process assumptions. We illustrate the method with an application to drug dosing personalization in Parkinson's disease. We use, as a simulator, a pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) model of a drug, a mechanistic model calibrated on population data with quantified parameter uncertainty. A dosing neural network is trained offline against the model, amortizing the map from patient PK/PD parameters to the regimen predicted to be optimal, and online Bayesian optimization then searches over this learned, low-dimensional representation rather than over raw dosages. Numerical simulations demonstrate the improved performance of our method in this application, as well as under simulator misspecification.

cross Anticipating Future Build Results with BuildPredictor

Authors: Christian Macho, Katharina Stengg

Abstract: Continuous Integration (CI) is a popular programming practice for the seamless integration of code changes into the code base. Using CI, developers benefit from automated assembling and testing of the code after each commit. However, while the frequent integration and building supports developers, it also comes at the high costs of wasting time and resources for minimal changes. In this work, we investigate the potential of reducing the drawbacks by anticipating the build outcome using machine learning techniques. cross Simple Private Noisy SGD is Minimax-Optimal under Population Strong Convexity

Authors: Valentin Roth, Marco Avella Medina

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is at the center of modern machine learning and is known to be statistically minimax-optimal in many regression settings, e.g., for convex Generalized Linear Models (GLMs). Yet, it is unknown whether its simplest differentially private Noisy SGD counterpart fulfills similar favorable guarantees even on strongly convex losses. We resolve this question affirmatively. We upper bound the parameter estimation error for a single pass of Noisy SGD with a fixed stepsize schedule and constant batch sizes on smooth and strongly convex Lipschitz losses over a compact convex domain under Renyi DP. cross Deterministic Temporal Embedding for Calcium Imaging Trace Extraction

Authors: Qi Gao, Julius Beichert, Miriam Ratliff, Karl Rohr

Abstract: Reliable extraction of calcium-dependent fluorescence traces in calcium imaging sequences requires identifying pixels whose fluorescence intensity reflects the same underlying cellular activity. Abstract: Large Web platforms operate large fleets of ranking models, each selecting features from a shared pool of tens of thousands. cross Text-Aware, Vision-Blind: How Multimodal Image Editing Systems Handle Unsafe Inputs

Authors: Ziqing Yang, Yixin Wu, Michael Backes, Yang Zhang

Abstract: Multimodal image generation systems have made rapid progress and increasingly emphasize content safety. However, their safety mechanisms tend to rely on a single modality and lack joint consideration of both visual content and text prompts, particularly in instruction-guided image editing, where the uploaded image may itself carry risk. We thus introduce EditSafetyBench, a benchmark that measures system-level safety awareness of advanced image editing systems, containing 5,940 image-instruction pairs, covering safe and unsafe images across 11 risk categories and three edit intents. Notably, sexual content shows the strongest visual sensitivity, likely benefiting from mature NSFW identification works. Our findings highlight the need for safety guardrails that jointly reason over both visual and textual modalities in image editing systems.

cross Represented but misread: Post-training changes how language models read out what they represent

Authors: Jochen Laubrock

Abstract: Does post-training change what a language model represents, or how this representation is read out into an answer? We compared linear probes with the models' own answers, analyzed with signal detection theory, in six open-weight base models and their post-trained versions. For judgments of whether German texts were written by a human or an AI, probes and word n-gram classifiers separated the classes well, but the answers did not and often ordered the texts inversely. Post-training lowered the sensitivity of the answers on average ($\Delta d' = -0.34$ across model families; -0.29 across three prompts). It also changed the use of a stated base rate: Base models shifted their log-odds by less than the stated change in prior odds, most post-trained models by more. In two models with stage-wise checkpoints, the change occurred during supervised fine-tuning, model stitching implicated the early layers, and from mid-depth on internal states at positions after the text, not those of the text, transferred the change between checkpoints. Our corpora cannot be shared, so we add a case reproducible from public data, the truth of negated statements. Here, post-training acted in the opposite direction: Probes decoded truth at or near ceiling in every model, the answers of several base models did not use it, and post-training repaired them; by mid-depth the repair resided at the positions after the statement; below it, the representation of the statement contributed, strongly in one of four models. In a training study, adding 2,000 correctly labeled authorship judgments of news texts to otherwise identical fine-tuning data improved the answers on our newspaper and textbook corpora and inverted them on the chats, where they followed a news-trained probe of the base model. Post-training thus changed how a judgment is read out of what a model represents, and a small training signal can set its direction.

cross Probing the "Conscious" Mind: Selective Access and Global Workspace in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Jiahao Huang, Jun Wang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) integrate visual and textual information, but their outputs do not reveal internal concept representations. Global workspace theory (GWT) proposes that information is selected from parallel brain processes, integrated in a shared workspace, and made available to specialised processes. We introduce the Multimodal Concept Lens (MC-Lens), a multimodal Jacobian lens, to study these functions in VLM concept processing. Its vision encoder and language decoder lenses map intermediate states to a shared semantic space defined by the decoder's output vocabulary to compare concept accessibility across modalities. Multimodal concept information is readable in intermediate states and selectively accessible as tasks require. We extend "readout carriers" from one name token to a token set to better capture and express concept information and support counterfactual experiments. (ii) Concepts in downstream computation. Counterfactual experiments show that accessed concept information participates in downstream computation and reasoning. (iii) Development of cross-modal alignment and selective access. We develop two J-space metrics tracking the development of cross-modal concept alignment more clearly than hidden state cosine in controlled VLM training. The experiment also shows that reliable selective access develops later than concept accessibility. (iv) Shared functions across models. Across VLM families and sizes, we find shared patterns of concept accessibility, query-dependent selectivity and cross-task intervention effects despite differences in internal organisation. These findings offer an experimental basis to study GWT in multimodal artificial systems and how it can guide cross-modal alignment improvements.

cross Realized versus Population Reliability: Anytime-Valid Self-Consistency Certification under Correlated Reasoning

Authors: Terence D. Agbeyegbe, Toneukarin G. Agbeyegbe

Abstract: Self-consistency certification treats repeated sampling from a language model as sequential evidence accumulation and stops when an anytime-valid e-process crosses a threshold, guaranteeing finite-sample Type I error control by Ville's inequality. Prior constructions assume sampled rollouts are conditionally independent. We show this assumption fails in practice: reasoning streams are positively correlated through shared reasoning prefixes or across-prompt heterogeneity, changing not merely certificate efficiency but the hypothesis certified. Modeling dependence via a per-unit latent state (the random-effects / intraclass-correlation structure) induces exchangeable, equi-correlated increments with correlation $\rho$. Correlation splits the single null into a realized null about the drawn belief and a population null about average model behavior, which coincide only when $\rho=0$. We show: (i) the standard betting e-process remains anytime-valid for the realized null, not the population null; (ii) read as a population-null test, a suitable mixture has asymptotic crossing probability at least $\pi=P(\mu(Z)>0)$, potentially exceeding nominal $\epsilon$; (iii) the within-unit mean's effective sample size saturates at $1/\rho$, so extra within-unit rollouts cannot restore population validity; and (iv) a prompt-level e-process restores population validity, with optimal allocation at one rollout per prompt under a pure rollout budget. Simulations and Claude Haiku 4.5 rollouts confirm this regime dependence: on GSM8K ($\hat{\rho}=0.435$, $\hat{\theta}=+0.96$) the gap is dormant, whereas on AIME ($\hat{\rho}=0.480$, $\hat{\theta}=-0.15$) the single-unit rule crosses on 24% of prompts at nominal $\epsilon=0.05$, while the prompt-level procedure declines (0.00). This correctness result identifies which reliability claim an anytime-valid certificate actually supports under dependence.

Authors: Peiyun Hu, Marko Mihajlovic, Lyndon Duong, Lahav Lipson, Wei Dong, Ama\"el Delaunoy, Stephan R. Richter, Vladlen Koltun

Abstract: We present Depth Pro 2, an approach to estimating dense metric depth for images and video at full resolution. By analyzing prior methods for monocular depth estimation, we find that they commonly trade off temporal consistency and boundary sharpness: multi-frame methods favor consistency, single-frame methods favor sharpness. To overcome this trade-off, we train a single, scalable network that processes both images and video and predicts depth maps at native resolution. Depth Pro 2 produces depth maps that are metrically accurate, sharp, and temporally consistent all at once, outperforming prior work in all of these dimensions. We release a family of models that define a new Pareto frontier in terms of metric accuracy, sharpness, temporal consistency, and runtime. We release code and weights at https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-depth-pro-2/

cross One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning

Authors: Yuxin Jiang, Wenqiang Sun, Jiaqi Li, Yuchao Gu, Lvmin Zhang, Ivor Tsang, Mike Zheng Shou

Abstract: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

cross TideCache: Efficient Hybrid LLM Serving with Transparent Cache Management and Layer-Wise Streaming

Authors: Jinbin Luo, Xiaozhe Yao, Shang Yang, Zhijian Liu, Song Han, Qinghao Hu

Abstract: Hybrid architectures have become mainstream among open-weight language models. Their global-attention and bounded-state layers maintain heterogeneous caches whose demands vary with context length and concurrency. We present TideCache, a serving system on CPU--GPU superchips with three components for allocating GPU memory among caches and managing their residency across GPU and host memory. The Transparent Cache Pool redistributes physical pages among cache components behind fixed virtual addresses, preserving cache layouts and backend interfaces. The Cache Streamer uses computation intervals provided by bounded-state layers to prefetch the next global-attention layer's prefix into GPU slots shared across global-attention layers, reducing GPU residency while overlapping layer-wise transfers with computation. Generated KV remains resident. The Request Scheduler selects resident or streamed execution and triggers capacity adjustments according to request size and queue state. Together, they let HBM released by streaming accommodate other cache components and additional requests. Implemented in SGLang and evaluated on GB300 and GH200 across seven model/platform configurations, TideCache completes up to 2.04x as many agent turns, reduces overall P50 per-token latency by 20--59%, and improves agent P50 TTFT by 1.40--3.05x compared with SGLang with HiCache.

cross RL Post-Training in Diffusion Transformers Is Activation Steering

Authors: Tuna Han Salih Meral, Kaan Oktay, Alec Helbling, Atakan Kara, Duen Horng Chau, Pinar Yanardag

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.

replace Security of Deep Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Driving: A Survey

Authors: Ambra Demontis, Srishti Gupta, Maura Pintor, Luca Demetrio, Kathrin Grosse, Hsiao-Ying Lin, Chengfang Fang, Battista Biggio, Fabio Roli

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) enables agents to learn optimal behaviors through interaction with their environment and has been increasingly deployed in safety-critical applications, including autonomous driving. Despite its promise, RL is susceptible to attacks designed either to compromise policy learning or to induce erroneous decisions by trained agents. Although the literature on RL security has grown rapidly and several surveys exist, existing categorizations often fall short in guiding the selection of appropriate defenses for specific systems. In this work, we present a comprehensive survey of 86 recent studies on RL security, addressing these limitations by systematically categorizing attacks and defenses according to defined threat models and single- versus multi-agent settings. replace Active Learning For Contextual Linear Optimization: A Margin-Based Approach

Authors: Mo Liu, Paul Grigas, Heyuan Liu, Zuo-Jun Max Shen

Abstract: We develop the first active learning method for contextual linear optimization. Specifically, we introduce a label acquisition algorithm that sequentially decides whether to request the ``labels'' of feature samples arriving in an unlabeled data stream, where the labels correspond to the objective coefficients in the linear optimization problem. The label acquisition rule is guided by the decision loss induced by the predicted coefficients, known as the Smart Predict-then-Optimize (SPO) loss. Motivated by the structure of the SPO loss, our algorithm uses the distance to degeneracy as a margin-based criterion for label acquisition. The algorithm processes incoming feature samples in batches, acquiring within each batch only the labels of samples whose distance to degeneracy falls below a rejection threshold. We derive non-asymptotic bounds for direct SPO-loss minimization over a general hypothesis class and for surrogate-loss minimization over a linear prediction class. These bounds demonstrate that, under certain natural conditions, our margin-based active learning algorithm requires fewer labels than supervised learning to achieve the same excess population risk for the chosen training loss. We provide concrete examples in which these conditions hold under continuous (sub-)Gaussian noise. Finally, we present numerical evidence demonstrating the practical value of our approach in applications such as personalized pricing and shortest-path problems.

replace FlexTSF: A Flexible Forecasting Model for Time Series with Variable Regularities

Authors: Jingge Xiao, Yile Chen, Gao Cong, Wolfgang Nejdl, Simon Gottschalk

Abstract: Generalizing across time series with irregular temporal structures poses a persistent challenge for universal time series forecasting models. Existing approaches often assume regular sampling or depend heavily on imputation, which limits their applicability in real-world scenarios where irregularities are prevalent due to diverse sensing devices and recording practices. To address this gap, we propose FlexTSF, a flexible and domain-adaptive forecasting model specifically designed for time series with variable temporal regularities. At its foundation lies the IVP Patcher, a continuous-time patching module leveraging Initial Value Problems (IVPs) to inherently handle uneven sampling intervals, variable sequence lengths, and missing values. Built upon a decoder-only Transformer architecture, FlexTSF integrates normalized timestamp inputs and domain-specific statistical descriptors through a specialized causal self-attention mechanism, enabling adaptability across domains. Extensive experiments on 16 datasets demonstrate FlexTSF's effectiveness, significantly outperforming existing models in classic forecasting scenarios, zero-shot generalization, and low-resource fine-tuning conditions.

replace Almost Sure Convergence Rates and Concentration of Stochastic Approximation and Reinforcement Learning with Markovian Noise

Authors: Xiaochi Qian, Zixuan Xie, Xinyu Liu, Shangtong Zhang

Abstract: This paper establishes, to our knowledge, the first almost sure convergence rate and the first maximal concentration bound with sub-Weibull tails for general contractive stochastic approximation algorithms with Markovian noise. As a corollary, we also obtain convergence rates in $L^p$. Key to our successes is a novel discretization of the mean ODE of stochastic approximation algorithms using intervals with diminishing (instead of constant) length. replace Learning with Local Search MCMC Layers

Authors: Germain Vivier-Ardisson, Mathieu Blondel, Axel Parmentier

Abstract: Integrating combinatorial optimization layers into neural networks has recently attracted significant research interest. However, many existing approaches lack theoretical guarantees or fail to perform adequately when relying on inexact solvers. This is a critical limitation, as many operations research problems are NP-hard, often necessitating the use of neighborhood-based local search heuristics. In this paper, we introduce a principled approach for learning with such inexact solvers. Inspired by the connection between simulated annealing and Metropolis-Hastings, we transform the problem specific neighborhood systems used in local search heuristics into proposal distributions, implementing MCMC on the set of feasible solutions. This allows us to construct differentiable, stochastic combinatorial layers and associated loss functions. Replacing an exact solver with a local search strongly reduces the computational burden of learning on many applications. We demonstrate our approach on a dynamic vehicle routing problem with time windows, binary vector and k-subset prediction tasks, as well as a multi-dimensional knapsack decision-focused learning problem.

Abstract: Hollmann et al. replace SCAR: State-Space Compression for AI-Based Radio Resource Management of Vehicular Services

Authors: Ioan-Sorin Comsa, Purav Shah, Karthik Vaidhyanathan, Deepak Gangadharan, Christof Imhof, Per Bergamin, Aryan Kaushik, Gabriel-Miro Muntean, Ramona Trestian

Abstract: The increasing demand for connected vehicular services poses significant challenges for AI-based network and service management due to the high volume and rapid variability of network state information. Traditional management and control mechanisms struggle to scale when processing fine-grained metrics such as Channel Quality Indicators (CQIs) in dynamic vehicular environments. To address this challenge, we propose SCAR (State-Space Compression for AI-Based Radio Resource Management), an edge-assisted framework that abstracts high-dimensional CQI information into compact representations for AI-based management. SCAR employs machine-learning (ML)-based compression techniques, including clustering and radial basis function (RBF) networks, to reduce the dimensionality of CQI-derived state information while preserving essential features relevant to management decisions. The resulting compressed states are used to train reinforcement learning (RL)-based management policies that dynamically adapt the throughput-fairness trade-off according to service-level fairness objectives defined by the NGMN. Simulation results show that CQI compression improves average NGMN feasible-region occupancy from 56.32% to 88.07% across ten RL algorithms, reaching 97.76% for the best-performing RL configuration, with limited throughput degradation and competitive computational overhead. Furthermore, simulated annealing with stochastic tunneling (SAST)-based clustering reduces state compression distortion by approximately 10% compared with conventional K-means clustering. These results demonstrate SCAR's ability to provide compact state representations for RL-based network and service management while improving service-level fairness in dynamic vehicular systems.

replace Benchmarking Robust Aggregation in Decentralized Gradient Marketplaces

Authors: Zeyu Song, Sainyam Galhotra, Shagufta Mehnaz

Abstract: Institutions that cannot centralize or sell raw data increasingly exchange model updates instead: cross-silo federated learning is already in production among hospital networks and pharmaceutical consortia. A growing line of research targets the next step: marketplaces in which gradient updates are exchanged as paid, query-specific data products, though none is yet deployed at scale. We formalize this emerging paradigm as a Gradient Data Marketplace (GDM), in which data consumers issue learning tasks and distributed participants contribute query-specific gradient updates. Federated learning asks whether the model survives an attack; a marketplace must also ask who got paid. Existing protocols ensure model-level robustness but fail to capture whether high-quality data is correctly identified and rewarded. In a GDM, selection determines payment, incentivizing participants to optimize for inclusion rather than utility. We introduce a modular benchmarking framework that separates mechanism integrity (whether selection and payment reward true utility) from model integrity, and evaluate a standard baseline alongside 11 widely used aggregation mechanisms on five real-world vision, text, and tabular datasets. Our results reveal consistent adverse selection: honest high-variance contributions are rejected as outliers while strategically homogeneous malicious contributions are accepted and rewarded, even when model performance remains stable. These failures are not artifacts of our accounting: they persist under continuous, weight-proportional payment, compound across repeated market rounds as underpaid honest sellers exit, and worsen when honest sellers add differential-privacy noise, which the filters misread as contamination. Our benchmark provides a foundation for designing incentive-aware, auditable data selection mechanisms in emerging data marketplaces.

replace Deconstructing Intraocular Pressure: A Non-Invasive Multi-Stage Probabilistic Inverse Framework

Authors: Md Rezwan Jaher, Abul Mukid Mohammad Mukaddes, A. B. M. Abdul Malek

Abstract: We propose the mechanistically hierarchical inverse problem (MHIP) paradigm for biological transport systems, where a macroscopic observation emerges from the multiplicative entanglement of intrinsic micro-scale physics and latent macro-scale geometry. Without in vivo ground truth, solving MHIPs from clinical data is severely underdetermined. Standard statistical sampling degenerates along non-identifiable manifolds, while monolithic machine learning baselines collapse to an uninterpretable conditional mean. We propose a two-stage physics-guided decomposition that turns a globally non-identifiable inverse mapping into conditionally identifiable sub-problems. The first stage is a constrained inverse surrogate that isolates the intrinsic parameter under a fixed reference geometry. The second stage performs empirical structural calibration against population-level clinical data. We also introduce Physics-Calibrated Data Scaling (PCDS), a multi-fidelity strategy that builds large training sets from few high-fidelity simulations without patient-specific 3D modeling. In a glaucoma case study, the framework deconstructs a single intraocular pressure (IOP) measurement to estimate trabecular permeability ($\hat{K}_{TM}$) and conventional outflow facility ($\hat{C}_{trab}$). In retrospective validation against published in vivo data, the non-invasive $\hat{C}_{trab}$ estimates showed low bias against measured outflow facility. In an ablation over 27 clinical cohorts, naive monolithic baselines reach 37.0% diagnostic accuracy. Adding the isolated $\hat{K}_{TM}$ estimate raises this to 85.2%. The full two-stage framework reaches 88.9%. The framework also returns a probabilistic profile of the latent hydrodynamic state, offering a general computational blueprint for MHIPs.

replace Extensions of Robbins-Siegmund Theorem with Applications in Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Xinyu Liu, Zixuan Xie, Shangtong Zhang

Abstract: The Robbins-Siegmund theorem establishes the convergence of stochastic processes that are almost supermartingales and is one of the most commonly used approaches for analyzing stochastic iterative algorithms in stochastic approximation and reinforcement learning (RL). However, its original form has a significant limitation as it requires the zero-order term to be summable. In many important RL applications, this summable condition, however, cannot be met. This limitation motivates us to extend the Robbins-Siegmund theorem for almost supermartingales where the zero-order term is not summable, but only square-summable. In particular, we introduce a novel and mild assumption on the increments of the stochastic processes. This together with the square-summable condition enables an almost sure convergence to a bounded set. Additionally, we further provide almost sure convergence rates, high probability concentration bounds, and $L^p$ convergence rates. We then apply the new results to stochastic approximation and RL. Notably, we obtain the first almost sure convergence rate, the first high probability concentration bound, and the first $L^p$ convergence rate for $Q$-learning with linear function approximation.

replace A Byzantine-Robust Federated Learning with Nesterov's Momentum

Authors: Lihan Xu, Xiaoyi Fan, Gang Wang, Runhao Zeng, Xiping Hu, Yanjie Dong

Abstract: We investigate robust federated learning, where a group of workers collaboratively train a shared model under the orchestration of a central server in the presence of Byzantine adversaries capable of arbitrary and potentially malicious behaviors. To improve communication efficiency while retaining resilience against such adversaries, we propose a Byzantine-resilient Nesterov-augmented federated learning (Byrd-NAFL) algorithm. Byrd-NAFL applies Nesterov's momentum at the server to the output of any soft $\gamma$-Byzantine resilient aggregation rule, while each worker only computes and uploads a stochastic gradient, which keeps the workers stateless, as desired for intermittently participating Internet of Things devices. For smooth non-convex losses, we establish a finite-time convergence guarantee for all momentum factors $\beta\in[0,1)$ without requiring the aggregated gradient to be unbiased or bounded; the bound reduces to that of Byzantine-resilient stochastic gradient descent at $\beta=0$ and is tighter than that of server-side heavy-ball momentum. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Sentiment140 show that Byrd-NAFL converges faster and attains higher accuracy than existing benchmarks in most i.i.d. and non-i.i.d. setups under diverse attacks, while its resilience is bounded by that of the underlying aggregation rule.

Abstract: Many travel decisions involve a degree of experience formation, where individuals learn their preferences over time. replace The Post-Training Dilemma: Why We Should Rethink the Sequential SFT-RL Paradigm

Authors: Xueyan Niu, Bo Bai, Wei Han, Weixi Zhang

Abstract: This position paper argues that the sequential supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning (SFT-then-RL) pipeline---the de facto paradigm for large language model (LLM) post-training---has a fundamental property that cannot be fixed by hyperparameter tuning: SFT and RL optimize over nearly orthogonal parameter subspaces, so RL inevitably degrades the capabilities acquired during the preceding SFT phase. Under the Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition, which is empirically observed for over-parameterized networks, we show that RL increases the SFT loss by at least $\frac{\mu}{2}\mathrm{dist}(\theta_{\mathrm{RL}}, \Theta^*_{\mathrm{SFT}})^2$ for some $\mu>0$. Although the KL penalty can constrain distributional shift, it cannot correct the underlying gradient misalignment between the two objectives. Experiments on Qwen3-0.6B across four tasks confirm the theoretical predictions: on MATH-500, Alpaca, HH-RLHF, and SST-2, the SFT loss increases after the RL phase. We identify the underlying mechanism as persistent near-orthogonality between the gradients of the two objectives, paired with opposing landscape dynamics: SFT flattens the loss landscape while RL re-sharpens it. Based on our analysis, we propose three promising research directions: (1) joint optimization of the two objectives with gradient surgery to resolve conflicting updates; (2) landscape-aware scheduling that terminates RL training at the theoretically optimal point; and (3) characterizing the model scale threshold above which SFT becomes unnecessary.

replace On the Complexity of Offline Reinforcement Learning with Q*-Approximation and Partial Coverage

Authors: Haolin Liu, Braham Snyder, Chen-Yu Wei

Abstract: We study offline reinforcement learning under $Q^\star$-approximation and partial coverage, a setting that motivates practical algorithms such as Conservative $Q$-Learning (CQL; Kumar et al., 2020) but has received limited theoretical attention. Our work is inspired by the following open question: "Are $Q^\star$-realizability and Bellman completeness sufficient for sample-efficient offline RL under partial coverage?" We answer in the negative via an information-theoretic lower bound. To identify additional structure that enables sample-efficient offline RL under partial coverage, we introduce a general decision-estimation framework, inspired by model-free decision-estimation coefficients (DEC) for online RL (Foster et al., 2023b; Liu et al., 2025b). Our framework decomposes offline RL complexity into decision complexity and value estimation error. This allows modular study of both sub-problems. Our result not only unifies existing results (Chen and Jiang, 2022; Uehara et al., 2023), but further improves and generalizes them. On the decision complexity side, our improvement includes: the first $\epsilon^{-2}$ sample complexity bound for soft $Q$-learning under partial coverage that improves Uehara et al.'s (2023) $\epsilon^{-4}$ bound, the removal of the need for additional online interaction in the value-gap setting of Chen and Jiang (2022), and new learnable settings beyond the above two cases. On the value estimation side, we provide a new characterization of the role of Bellman completeness under partial coverage, and the first characterization of offline learnability for general low-Bellman-rank MDPs (Jiang et al., 2017; Du et al., 2021; Jin et al., 2021). The latter is a canonical online RL setting that has remained unexplored in offline RL except for special cases. As a side contribution, our techniques give the first analysis of CQL in the function approximation setting.

Abstract: Learning systems must balance generalization across experiences with discrimination of task-relevant details. Effective learning therefore requires representations that support both.
