replace Friction-Augmented Drifting Models for Resource-Efficient Domain Translation

Authors: Arkadii Kazanskii, Tatiana Petrova, Andrey Ustyuzhanin, Konstantin Bagrianskii, Aleksandr Puzikov, Radu State

Abstract: Drifting Models (DMs) train a one-step generator by regressing its output towards a target displaced by a kernel drift. DMF (Drifting Model with Friction) scales this displacement linearly from full drift to zero. With plain gradient descent, scaling the drift gives the same updates as linear learning-rate decay. With Adam, it also changes the gradient moments. For a constant multiplier $c>0$, its effect in exact arithmetic is to replace Adam's $\epsilon$ by $\epsilon/c$. In a two-particle model with unnormalised Laplace interactions, we derive the distance at which attraction balances repulsion. On FFHQ adult-to-child translation, DMF lowered FID and CMMD relative to fixed-rate DM in all five seed pairs. In a separate paired comparison, mean FID and CMMD were lower for DM with linear learning-rate decay than for DMF. Both 95% intervals for the paired differences included zero. DMF inherits DM's low training cost. At batch size 32 on one RTX A6000, mean training times were 6.85 minutes for DMF, 6.93 for DM and 133.48 for Optimal Flow Matching (OFM). At this batch size, DMF also achieved lower FID and CMMD than OFM (paired $p=0.002$ and $0.021$, respectively).

replace FRInGe: Distribution-Space Integrated Gradients with Fisher--Rao Geometry

Authors: Gabriele Martino, Sebastian Tschiatschek

Abstract: Although gradient-based attribution methods are model-faithful and scalable, a prominent variant, Integrated Gradients (IG), suffers from high sensitivity to design choices, particularly in baseline selection, path formulation, and discretization, as well as gradient saturation. To address these limitations, we propose Fisher--Rao Integrated Gradients (FRInGe), which defines both the reference and integration paths in the predictive distribution space. FRInGe replaces input baselines with a maximum-entropy predictive reference and follows a Fisher--Rao geodesic on the probability simplex. The corresponding input-space trajectory is realized through the pullback Fisher metric and stabilized by Kullback--Leibler (KL) and Euclidean trust regions; attributions are obtained by integrating input gradients along this trajectory. We further derive FRInGe-B, a variant that binarizes the predictive probability distribution, removing the computationally expensive Fisher products of FRInGe. Across six ImageNet architectures, FRInGe most clearly improves Magnitude Aligned Scoring (MAS), while remaining competitive on perturbation area-under-the-curve (AUC) metrics and infidelity.

replace Spherical Boltzmann machines: a solvable theory of learning and generation in energy-based models

Authors: Thomas Tulinski, Simona Cocco, R\'emi Monasson, Jorge Fernandez-De-Cossio-Diaz

Abstract: Energy-based models (EBMs) are flexible generative architectures inspired by statistical physics, but their learning and generative properties remain poorly understood. Here, we analyze a solvable EBM in the high-dimensional limit: the spherical Boltzmann machine (SBM). Combining tools from random matrix theory and dynamical mean-field theory, we: derive and solve closed equations describing the training dynamics of the SBM in the high-dimensional limit; compute the asymptotic Bayesian evidence, which acts as a partition function in parameter space and encodes global properties of the trained model; and uncover cascades of phase transitions that occur both during training and as a function of hyperparameters, related to successive alignment and condensation of the top modes of the coupling matrix to the data. We connect these transitions to sampling-time generative phenomena in a teacher-student scenario, including: sampling temperature tuning, double descent as a function of regularization strength, tempered posterior effects, and out-of-equilibrium effects during training that induce biases in the trained model. We provide numerical evidence showing that all these phenomena appear in standard generative architectures, beyond the SBM.

replace TriAxialKV: Toward Extreme Low-Precision KV-Cache Quantization for Agentic Inference Tasks

Authors: Hanzhang Shen, Haoran Wu, Yiren Zhao, Robert Mullins

Abstract: Agentic workloads have emerged as a major workload for LLM inference. They differ significantly from chat-only workloads, requiring long-context processing, the ability to handle multimodal inputs, and structured multi-turn interactions with tool calling capabilities. As a result, their context exhibits structure that can carry different importance along three key axes: temporal recency to the current turn, modality such as text or image tokens, and semantic role such as user queries, tool calls, observations, or reasoning. These axes capture distinct token behaviors and lead to different sensitivities to KV-cache compression. However, existing KV-cache quantization methods are typically homogeneous or exploit only heterogeneity on a single dimension, such as temporal proximity or modality, overlooking the interactions among them. To this end, we introduce TriAxialKV, a novel mixed-precision KV-cache quantization scheme that assigns each token a triaxial tag, calibrates per-tag sensitivity, and allocates INT2/INT4 bit-widths under a fixed memory budget. We implement TriAxialKV as an end-to-end serving system, comprising calibration, mixed-precision quantization and memory management, and custom fused Triton decode kernels. When using Qwen3-VL-32B-Thinking as a computer-use agent operating the OSWorld, TriAxialKV matches the accuracy of SGLang with BF16 KV cache while supporting 4.5$\times$ KV cache capacity and achieving 30% higher end-to-end throughput, when running on real GPU systems.

replace AVSD: Adaptive-View Self-Distillation by Balancing Consensus and Teacher-Specific Privileged Signals

Authors: Duy Nguyen, Hanqi Xiao, Archiki Prasad, Zaid Khan, Anirban Das, Shi-Xiong Zhang, Sambit Sahu, Hyunji Lee, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal

Abstract: Self-distillation enables language models to learn on-policy from their own trajectories by using the same model as both student and teacher, with the teacher being conditioned on privileged information unavailable to the student. Such information can come in different types or views, such as solutions, demonstrations, feedback, or final answers. This setup provides dense token-level feedback without relying on a separate external model, but creates a fundamental asymmetry: the teacher may rely on view-specific information that the student cannot access at inference time. Moreover, the best type of privileged information is often task-dependent, making it difficult to choose a single teacher view. In this work, we address both these challenges jointly by introducing AVSD (Adaptive-View Self-Distillation), a novel method of self-distillation with multiple privileged-information views, which reconstructs token-level supervision by separating stable cross-view consensus from view-specific residual signals. AVSD identifies the consensus signal shared across views, which provides a reliable update direction, and then selectively adds the view-specific residual signal to adjust the update magnitude when it both aligns with the consensus direction and remains proportionate to the consensus signal. Experiments on math competition benchmarks (AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25) show that AVSD consistently outperforms both single-view self-distillation baselines and GRPO, achieving average Avg@8 gains of 3.1% and 2.2% over the strongest baselines on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B, respectively. Moreover, on code-generation benchmarks (Codeforces, LiveCodeBench v6) using Qwen3-8B, AVSD outperforms the single-view self-distillation baseline by 2.4% on average.

replace Learning Which Evidence-Seeking Decisions Matter: Calibrated Counterfactual Replay for Language Agents

Authors: Renwei Meng, Chengyi Sun, Hongyi Zhang, Xiaoyi Hou

Abstract: Language agents answer questions by searching, reading, and checking claims. A reward on the final answer says little about which of those decisions earned it, since an agent can reach the right string after redundant searches or with half the evidence missing. We treat the question as an experiment. At a saved decision point, PCCC-RL, for prefix-conditioned counterfactual credit, restores the tool state, runs the action the agent chose alongside a feasible alternative, and lets the same frozen policy finish both branches. The difference in final score then samples what the decision was worth. Replaying everything is too expensive, so the method predicts each action's credit up front, checks a random subset, and uses the recorded selection probabilities to correct the errors of that prediction, spending the budget where a further check can still move the training signal. Alternatives are drawn from the prefix alone, which keeps the comparison a usable gradient baseline. On HotpotQA and MuSiQue, joint exact match, requiring a correct answer together with complete supporting evidence, reaches 56.71% and 36.82%, gains of 4.19 and 3.86 points over a baseline with the same replay access. The gain concentrates in complete evidence chains and accumulates over many updates. Single-update effects stay uncertain, and citation coverage and scope distinctions such as "none" versus "not all" remain open.

replace SPACE: Source-free Proxy Anchor Concept Erasure for MLLMs

Authors: Zhijing Zhang, Jiaqi Ding, Qianshan Wei, Nan Zhou, Jiaqi Li, Yongliang Wu, Tongxin Zhu, Xiaolin Fang

Abstract: As Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face growing privacy risks and regulatory constraints, machine unlearning (MU) has emerged as a crucial solution for removing sensitive data while preserving model performance. However, existing MU methods typically rely on visual data of the target concepts, which is often unavailable due to strict data retention policies, thus creating a demand for source-free unlearning approaches that operate without access to the target data. In this work, we propose Source-free Proxy Anchor Concept Erasure (SPACE), the first source-free unlearning framework specialized for MLLMs. SPACE consists of two stages: (1) Text-Guided Proxy Anchor Selection (TPAS), which retrieves semantically aligned proxy anchors from the shared feature space. (2) Dual-Constraint Semantic Isolation (DCSI), which optimizes these anchors to indirectly erase target concepts. DCSI confines updates to the null space of retained knowledge, ensuring structural integrity. We theoretically prove that SPACE strictly bounds the perturbation on retained knowledge and maximizes feature spectral entropy, thereby maintaining the model's performance. Furthermore, extensive experiments across six datasets show that SPACE achieves performance comparable to that of state-of-the-art data-dependent methods, validating its effectiveness in source-free MU scenarios. The source code will be released.

replace RePAIR: Predictive Self-Supervised Representation Learning in Chess

Authors: Christoph Koller, Johannes F\"urnkranz, Timo Bertram

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce Representation Prediction via Autoencoding using Iterative Refinement (RePAIR) - a novel self-supervised representation learning architecture that synthesizes Masked Autoencoders (MAE), Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA), and Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT). We demonstrate how it can be used to encode objects in sequential data like consecutive chess positions into compact yet meaningful representations. The basic principle of the architecture is to mask large portions of a sequence of latent states, similar to BERT and MAE. Then, we apply a lightweight Predictor to the latent representations that repairs gaps in the sequence in a lower-dimensional embedding space akin to JEPA. Our experiments in the domain of chess show that the Encoder refines the board representations such that meaningful chess concepts emerge clustered in the latent space. Furthermore, reconstructions of the masked board states show that the model is able to reason about the piece movements without relying on costly reinforcement learning methods. Lastly, we find that the resulting representation space allows for quick and intuitive dissections of chess games by observing the game path trajectories in this semantically rich space.

replace Implicit Neural Representations of Individual Behavior

Authors: Andrew Kang, Priya Narasimhan

Abstract: We study policy representation learning from unlabeled multi-policy behavioral data. Each episode is generated by a fixed policy, but policy labels are unavailable. This setting appears in robotics play, demonstrations, games, racing, and other datasets where heterogeneous behaviors are mixed without annotations. We introduce Behavioral INR, a self-supervised generative model that adapts implicit neural representations (INRs) from vision to behavior. Instead of mapping coordinates to RGB values, Behavioral INR represents a policy as a state-action function mapping states to subsequent actions. An episode-level latent modulates this function through FiLM layers, yielding a generative prior over policies and allowing policy identity to be inferred without supervision. Because INRs treat each datapoint as samples from an underlying function, the same model naturally accommodates variable episode lengths and different sampling granularities, as in vision INRs with different image resolutions. We also define policy-level out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts along state-distribution and action-distribution axes, which arise when policies overlap in states or actions but are not captured by standard behavioral OOD settings based only on new agents or environments. We evaluate on synthetic Gaussian random field data, MuJoCo demonstrations with controlled OOD splits, and real-world chess, Formula 1 racing, robotics, and Seek-Avoid datasets. Behavioral INR most consistently improves policy identifiability in the hardest continuous state-action settings, especially when longer episodes, more policies, and OOD splits reduce the usefulness of marginal shortcuts; amortized history encoders remain competitive when policy identity can be recovered from symbolic repetition or low-dimensional action statistics.

replace Rational Sparse Autoencoder

Authors: Naiyu Yin, Maosen Tang, Alex Townsend, Yue Yu

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are standard tools for mechanistic interpretability, but current SAE families are constrained by fixed encoder nonlinearities such as ReLU, JumpReLU, and TopK. This hard-codes a particular sparsity mechanism into the model and can distort the reconstruction-versus-sparsity trade-off. We introduce the Rational Sparse Autoencoder (RSAE), which replaces the fixed encoder activation with a trainable rational function. The design is motivated by a reconstruction advantage that we prove for RSAE at matched expected sparsity, with guaranteed non-inferiority to ReLU and, under stated assumptions, provable improvements over JumpReLU and TopK. The model is then implemented through a two-stage pipeline: an initialization procedure that copies the pre-trained baseline SAE weights and calibrates the scale parameters along with the rational coefficients; followed by a fine-tuning step under a sparsity-regularized reconstruction objective that holds $\ell_0$ at the teacher's value. Consistent with this theory, on residual-stream activations of three open-weight language models and across all three baseline activation families, the RSAE improves reconstruction fidelity after the fine-tuning step at the same level of sparsity. These gains are consistent across host language models, across baseline activation families, and across the full range of baseline sparsity we tested, while the upgrade itself adds only 20 scalar parameters per autoencoder and one training run takes between about 4 and 25 minutes on a single consumer GPU.

replace When Does Depth Matter For In-Context Learning? Adaptive Inference in Deep Transformers

Authors: Ravin Raj, Gautam Reddy

Abstract: Transformers perform computations through many successive attention and feedforward blocks, allowing them to learn complex correlations between a large collection of coupled variables. When does stacking successive attention-feedforward computations over many layers provide a computational advantage over a single transformer block? We address this question by examining in-context learning in generalized linear attention transformers. We first introduce a general theory of distributed inference in such transformers, subject to constraints on communication and depth. We show that such systems can exploit internal representations (`function vectors') to infer a latent context variable at increasingly finer scales over its layers. For an in-context linear regression task, the theory predicts that while one-layer transformers without feedforward blocks are optimal for Gaussian priors over the context variable, multi-layer transformers are superior for non-Gaussian, tree-like priors. Trained linear attention transformers reproduce quantitative predictions from the theory. Using causal key-patching experiments, we verify that function vectors in intermediate layers mediate adaptive routing of information. Our results suggest that depth and feedforward blocks enable transformers to implement adaptive inference, and this is advantageous when the distribution over latent variables has hierarchical structure.

replace A human-in-the-loop Bayesian optimization framework for constraint-aware bioprocess development

Authors: Samuel Stricker, Claus Wirnsperger, Alessandro Butt\'e, Laura Marie Helleckes, Gonzalo Guill\'en Gos\'albez, Antonio del Rio Chanona, Mehmet Mercang\"oz

Abstract: We present Pareto Front Guided Sampling (PFGS), a human-in-the-loop (HiL) Bayesian Optimization (BO) framework that reformulates surrogate-model predictions as a multi-objective optimization problem, allowing domain knowledge to beadded intuitively to any BO problem. PFGS addresses constrained optimization by treating the posterior probability of constraint satisfaction as an explicit Pareto objective, and robust optimization via a Monte Carlo estimate of expected lower-confidence performance under input perturbations, capturing likely degradation. The multi-dimensional Pareto front makes trade-offs between predicted performance, model uncertainty, constraint satisfaction, and robustness visible through pairwise two-dimensional projections on an interactive dashboard, allowing selection criteria to be refined as the model and objectives evolve. The framework is showcased on a eight-dimensional in-silico fed-batch Chinese Hamster Ovary cell culture case study. We showed improved performance over BO when the expert has perfect domain knowledge; however, PFGS is not designed to outperform BO, but rather to present a HiL acquisition function framework.

replace Hierarchical Pooling for Sheaf Neural Networks

Authors: Dionisia Naddeo, Carlo Abate, Pietro Li\`o, Nicola Toschi, Filippo Maria Bianchi

Abstract: Sheaf neural networks (SNNs) extend graph neural networks by operating on cellular sheaves, which assign to each node and edge its own vector space, along with linear restriction maps from node spaces to the spaces associated with their incident edges. These maps determine the sheaf Laplacian and can be learned at each layer, enabling layer-specific diffusion dynamics. Existing SNN architectures, however, always diffuse on the same graph, with restriction maps that may change across layers but continue to act on the original nodes and edges. We introduce Hierarchical Sheaf Pooling (HiSP), a pooling layer that enables hierarchical representation learning in SNNs. Following the Select-Reduce-Connect-Lift framework, HiSP groups fine-scale nodes, reduces their features using the fine-scale sheaf structure, and constructs the corresponding coarse graph. A new sheaf is then learned at the coarse resolution. By interleaving sheaf diffusion and pooling, HiSP enables hierarchical SNNs to learn distinct diffusion dynamics across layers and graph resolutions. This construction supports multiscale problems in which aggregation changes both the interacting entities and their relations.

replace Necessary, Not Sufficient, and No Longer Available: Grader Non-Determinism After the Withdrawal of Sampling Controls

Authors: Hiroki Tamba

Abstract: LLM-as-judge ("grader") components are now standard in evaluation harnesses, including safety evaluations where a pass/fail verdict may gate downstream deployment decisions. Earlier work, my own included, treated temperature=0 as the remedy for grader non-determinism and asked whether it was sufficient. It was necessary and not sufficient. It is now also unavailable. I report a two-vendor measurement taken in October and November 2026. Anthropic's current models reject the setting outright, and under the API version dated 2026-10-21 all fifteen models listed for my key reject it, the older generations included. On the chat completions endpoint OpenAI's current models accept the parameter and refuse every value but one, so a harness grading through that endpoint is obliged to sample its judge at temperature 1. On the Responses endpoint a request carrying temperature=0 is accepted by four of five current models once reasoning is switched off, a configuration I have not graded under, and by gpt-6.1-sol under no setting I tried. I propose the measurement that replaces the earlier protocol: the per-item normalized entropy of the grade distribution, reported at whatever setting the vendor permits. The direct alternative is closed too: gpt-4o returns token log-probabilities, while gpt-5.6-sol refuses the parameter and the Anthropic Messages API does not accept it at all. Across twenty-two condition runs and 4,760 calls the index orders graders without requiring a rate estimate, and identifies items at which a judge retains no information at all. I further show that a borderline item set carries across model generations only in part and not predictably, so a harness cannot inherit its calibration set. I argue that grader disagreement must be reported as an error bar on every verdict, because it is no longer something an evaluator can configure away.

replace Persistent Sparse Autoencoders: Learning Feature-Specific Timescales in Language Model Representations

Authors: Haoyan Luo, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Mateja Jamnik

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language model activations into sparse features, yet these models traditionally encode each token independently, failing to expose information that persists across a sequence. We first show that temporal persistence can naturally emerge in standard SAE features: after a feature activates, the hidden state remains aligned with its direction, and past activations reduce the reconstruction error of later hidden states. How long this lasts varies widely across features. We therefore introduce Persistent Sparse Autoencoders (Persistent SAEs), an extension of standard SAEs that learns a persistence coefficient for each feature, allowing feature-specific timescales to emerge from reconstruction. Our experiments show that Persistent SAEs retain competitive reconstruction quality while learning a spectrum of timescales: short-timescale (fast) features remain locally interpretable, whereas long-timescale (slow) features accumulate higher-level contextual information. Moreover, we show in a prompt-injection monitoring case study that slow features retain injection signals over long contexts and causally affect monitor judgements. These results suggest that Persistent SAEs offer new opportunities for interpreting and monitoring language models via persistent features.

replace From field-scale to large-scale spectral libraries: Tabular foundation models in soil spectroscopy

Authors: Viacheslav Barkov, Jonas Schmidinger, Robin Gebbers, Martin Atzmueller

Abstract: Visible and near-infrared (vis-NIR) and mid-infrared (MIR) spectroscopy enable rapid, cost-effective prediction of soil properties. Yet, translating high-dimensional, highly collinear spectra into accurate soil property predictions remains challenging, particularly when employing machine learning. We systematically investigated regression models and dimensionality reduction approaches for spectroscopic modeling across 85 soil property prediction tasks from open benchmark datasets in pedometrics, spanning field-scale digital soil mapping and a global soil spectral library. We compared an in-context learning tabular foundation model (TabPFN), a convolutional neural network (CNN), rule-based regression (Cubist), Random Forest, and partial least squares regression (PLSR) using full spectra as well as features derived from principal component analysis (PCA) and partial least squares (PLS) latent variables. TabPFN consistently delivered the best overall performance across spectral regions, properties, and scales, including large spectral library tasks with tens of thousands of soil samples. Notably, TabPFN applied directly to full spectra already surpassed all classical baselines, showing that explicit dimensionality reduction is not strictly required for strong performance. Further improvements were achieved through PLS, which proved to be an effective dimensionality reduction strategy for all models. Combining PLS latent variables with TabPFN yielded the best predictions overall, lowering the root mean squared error (RMSE) relative to the strongest classical baseline on 44 of the 46 large-scale spectral library tasks, by a median of 14%. Our findings provide evidence-based guidance for spectroscopic calibration model selection across operational scales, demonstrating that the long-standing advantages of PLSR and modern tabular foundation models complement each other in chemometrics.

replace Omni-modal decomposition autoencoders learn full-stack wearable disentangled representations

Authors: Ioannis Ziogas, Ensieh Khazaei, Bilal Taha, Aamna Al Shehhi, Ahsan H. Khandoker, Leontios J. Hadjileontiadis, Dimitrios Hatzinakos

Abstract: Learning disentangled representations is key for general-purpose multi-modal wearable models, yet existing approaches rarely combine classification, disentanglement, fusion, and generation of heterogeneous multi-modal time series in a single model. OmniDecVAEs extend DecVAEs by learning time-frequency latent subspaces per modality through a multi-view self-supervised decomposition loss and a component-wise conditional decoder. On the omni-modal human activity recognition (HAR) HARWE dataset (up to thirty channels) and the public UCI HAR and WESAD datasets, OmniDecVAEs learn the most disentangled representations for unseen subjects (DCI disentanglement up to 3.0 percentage points (pp) higher). replace Two Principles for Autoregressive Language Modeling

Authors: Runlin Shi, Bojian Yin, Guoqi Li

Abstract: We investigate the functional differentiation learned by RoPE Transformers to derive principles for autoregressive language modeling. We introduce two intervention-based metrics: RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS) measures how removing a frequency contribution changes a head's full attention distribution, and RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD) isolates the effect of rotary modulation. In Qwen3 and Llama3.1, these metrics distinguish retrieval from positional modeling, while individual heads can exhibit both functions. A mid-low-frequency Global Positional Band (GPBand) separates their frequency dependencies and tracks the training sequence length in controlled experiments, suggesting that global positional fitting can hinder zero-shot length extrapolation. These findings motivate two principles. (1) Functional Organization: retrieval and positional functions should be assigned separately and mixed within layers with layer-specific allocation; and (2) Positional Dependence: positional modeling should operate only locally, while global access should rely on position-independent retrieval. Matched pretraining from scratch and targeted architectural ablations at 380M and 1.4B parameters support both principles and highlight the importance of mechanism choice in realizing each function. Together, these findings suggest a promising route toward architectural innovation grounded in the functional requirements of autoregressive language modeling.

replace Fast Gauss Sums via Flash Attention

Authors: Nicolaj Rux, Sebastian Neumayer

Abstract: Gauss kernel sums are the computational core of maximum mean discrepancies (MMDs), kernel gradient flows, Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD), and many other kernel methods. At the same time, softmax attention has received an extraordinary amount of hardware-aware code engineering, culminating in FLASH attention. We show that Gauss kernel sums with arbitrary, signed weights can be evaluated via FLASH attention: two small input augmentations turn the normalized softmax reduction into the unnormalized Gauss sum, without writing a single line of custom GPU code. replace Prior-Amortized In-Context Bayesian Inference for Generalized Linear Mixed-Effects Models

Authors: Alex Kipnis, Marcel Binz, Eric Schulz

Abstract: Hierarchical data is ubiquitous in the empirical sciences and most commonly analyzed with generalized linear mixed-effects models (GLMMs). Bayesian inference for GLMMs yields calibrated uncertainty estimates but requires Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC). The gold-standard No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) is slow and must restart from scratch for every new dataset, model and prior specification. We introduce MetaBeta, a pretrained amortized model for in-context Bayesian inference over GLMMs. The full prior and model specification are supplied as context at test time, and the model returns an asymptotically exact posterior at the speed of a forward pass. Two set transformers and two conditional neural spline flows mirror the GLMM's hierarchical structure and are trained jointly on millions of realistic simulated datasets with continuous, binary and count outcomes. The flow posterior is refined by Independence Metropolis-Hastings against the unnormalized posterior. This yields tuning-free asymptotically correct inference. Alternatively, MetaBeta can warm-start NUTS. This gives nearly identical inference 3.5-13x faster than cold-started NUTS and removes over 98% of NUTS' divergent transitions. On controlled benchmarks with ground-truth parameters, MetaBeta matches NUTS in parameter recovery, posterior calibration and out-of-sample prediction. On real datasets with unknown parameters, priors and model structure, its posteriors closely agree with those of NUTS. Furthermore, it remains faithful where amortized models typically struggle: under misspecified likelihoods, misspecified priors, out-of-distribution predictors, collinear designs and data-poor regimes. On real data, a full prior sensitivity analysis including model comparison takes a few seconds with MetaBeta against 14-18 core-hours for NUTS, tracing its posteriors throughout. Our model is open-source and open-weights.

replace Learning from Transients: Linear RNN Scaling Laws in Stationary Prediction

Authors: Ziyan Chen, Linhe Zheng, Peilin Liu, Zhongzhu Zhou, Ding-Xuan Zhou

Abstract: Does the organization of a fixed number of transitions into independent trajectories affect a fixed stationary prediction task? We study a Gaussian-sketched linear recurrent teacher--student model, where independently initialized training trajectories create transients that can excite weak spectral directions. For full-batch gradient descent with stable deterministic learning rates, we establish matching stationary approximation, bias, variance, and total-risk scaling laws from dependent trajectory data. When initialization decays at least as fast spectrally as the innovations, trajectory count and length enter the leading variance rate through their product. With a heavier initialization tail, transient excitation can improve the trajectory-length-dependent finite-time bias and, at fixed transition count, variance, while the stationary oracle shift remains controlled by representation approximation. Under token and compute caps, constant-length trajectories attain the optimal risk scaling order within the theorem-controlled range. Synthetic experiments provide finite-scale evidence in this stylized model.

replace Chameleon: Dynamic Format Adapter for Efficient Diffusion

Authors: Arnab Sanyal, Sandeep Chinchali

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is the standard way to run modern diffusion models on memory-constrained accelerators, yet every existing diffusion PTQ scheme fixes the $\mathit{number\ format}$ in advance and only tunes the scale, zero point, or per-layer bit-width. At a fixed bit-width the best format depends on the distribution being encoded, and that distribution differs across weight channels, across layers, and along the diffusion timestep, where activation distributions slide from heavy-tailed and noise-dominated to tightly clustered and structured. We propose Chameleon, a PTQ framework that holds the bit-width fixed and treats the format itself as a discrete variable, chosen per weight channel and per activation layer, optionally refined per timestep bucket. Activation formats come from {INT8, FP8 E4M3, FP8 E5M2, MXFP8, MXINT8}, selected ahead of time from two cheap statistics (empirical kurtosis and the closed-form diffusion SNR) and stored in a lookup table; weight formats come from {INT8, MXINT8} at 8 bits or {INT4, NF4, FP4 E2M1, MXINT4, MXFP4} at 4 bits, selected offline by reconstruction error. An architectural fork adapts the same selection layer to multi-step UNets, single-step distilled models, and Diffusion Transformers. Most of the gain comes from the per-layer choice; the timestep refinement helps mainly at 6-bit activations. Across SDXL, SDXL-Turbo, and PixArt-$\alpha$ on COCO-2014, Chameleon achieves the best FID in all six backbone $\times$ bit-width settings, with CLIP within 0.24 of the FP16 reference and the best of all quantized methods at $W_{4}A_{8}$. On PixArt-$\alpha$, a real mixed-format kernel runs 1.09$\times$ faster than FP16 end-to-end with 3$\times$ smaller weights.

replace ProtoSeam: Lifting Classifier Training with Latent Gaussian Mixture Models

Authors: Robert Lampel, Timon Klein, Sebastian Sager

Abstract: We propose a lifted reformulation of supervised classification that improves the final accuracy of standard classifiers without changing the architecture at inference time. A network $N=N_2\circ N_1$ is split at a single semantic interface and one learnable prototype per class is inserted there. Training combines a quadratic consensus penalty that pulls $N_1(x)$ toward the prototype of its class with a classification loss of $N_2$ evaluated on samples drawn around the prototypes, while no gradient crosses the interface. At inference the prototypes are discarded and the unmodified network $N_2\circ N_1$ is used. Across CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet with ResNet and vision transformer backbones, lifted training improves test accuracy by up to six percentage points over variants without lifting under a shared tuning protocol. Moreover, we provide theoretical justification of those results.

replace Tetra: Serving Leech-Lattice Quantized LLMs at 2.7 Bits per Parameter

Authors: Pier-Jean Malandrino

Abstract: Leech-lattice quantization gives good quality at two bits per weight, but its codebooks hold more than 10^14 points, too many for a lookup table. Our earlier kernel expanded the codes at load time and read 4.804 bits per weight from GPU memory for 2 bits of code. We present Tetra, a new codebook on the same lattice. A 24-weight block still takes 48 bits, most of which index a 64-state trellis of the Golay code and one shared 16 KiB table. The kernel decodes a block with six table loads and two small lookups inside the matrix-vector product, and reads 2.148 bits per weight. For full models, we retrain one scale per matrix row, store the matrices that lose the most as 4-bit integers, and pay for them with 4-bit embedding tables. Our Qwen3-4B, 8B and 14B files hold 2.73, 2.70 and 2.73 bits per parameter over the whole model. They score 63.37, 69.58 and 75.66 on the full MMLU test set, 4.76, 4.21 and 2.46 points below 4-bit AWQ at 5.3 to 6.0 bits per parameter. They generate 113.8, 95.0 and 57.2 tokens per second in our engine. On GSM8K, through the served kernel, they lose 9.63, 4.62 and 3.26 points to FP16. At 4B our file scores 23.6 points above llama.cpp's IQ2_XXS (2.48 bits per parameter). Every number we measured for a table or figure comes from one NVIDIA L40S GPU. We preregistered the main experiments.

replace Arbitrary-Accuracy Neural Approximation with Optimal Neuron Count and Optimal Bit Complexity

Authors: Zilan Cheng, Li-Lian Wang, Zhongjian Wang

Abstract: We study the minimum number of hidden neurons required for arbitrary-accuracy approximation of continuous functions on $[0,1]^d$ and the associated encoding complexity. For $d\geq 2$, we construct two fixed, explicitly defined activation functions and, for each, a closed-form network with two hidden layers of widths $d$ and $1$ that achieves arbitrary accuracy in the uniform norm. We prove that $d+1$ is the exact minimum total number of hidden neurons among standard feedforward networks with locally integrable activations and affine outputs. We further give a simpler construction using a single elementary activation that combines the floor and exponential functions. This construction requires three hidden layers of widths $d$, $1$, and $2$. If a skip connection is allowed, widths $d$, $1$, and $1$ suffice. For a bounded $\alpha$-H\"older class with $\alpha\in(0,1]$, the networks with the digit-extraction activation and with the elementary activation require $O(\varepsilon^{-d/\alpha}\log(1/\varepsilon))$ bits, matching the metric-entropy lower bound up to a logarithmic factor. The other $(d,1)$ network, which stores differences of neighboring values along a spanning tree, needs only $\Theta(\varepsilon^{-d/\alpha})$ bits and is optimal in both neuron count and bit complexity.

replace Channel-Dependent State Space Model for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Yu-Cheng Wu, Fan-Keng Sun, Li-Chun Lu, Duane S. Boning

Abstract: Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) is critical across many real-world domains. Existing deep learning approaches fall into two paradigms with distinct limitations: channel-independent (CI) methods ignore cross-variable dependencies and model only temporal dynamics, while channel-dependent (CD) methods consider both but typically rely on architectural compromises to mitigate overfitting and computational overhead. We therefore propose Chameleon, a CD state space model (SSM) that enables data-dependent, fine-grained interactions across variables while scaling linearly with the number of variables. By connecting selective SSMs with the Kalman filter, we adapt the latter's measurement-update formulation for cross-variable modeling while preserving the SSM backbone for robust temporal modeling. We further identify favorable inductive biases of GatedDeltaNet for time series, adapt it as our backbone, and improve generalization through additional techniques, including a previously unexplored stochastic perturbation of reversible instance normalization. On strongly dependent ODE and PEMS datasets, Chameleon achieves the best MSE and MAE across all settings, while its CI ablation and prior CD methods incur 61-178% higher MSE on average. Across 28 standard benchmark settings, Chameleon also achieves better MSE and MAE than each baseline in at least 27 and 22 cases, respectively. Training-time, FLOP, and peak-memory analyses on Traffic and ETT further demonstrate competitive efficiency and favorable scalability across different variable counts.

replace Clause-Factored Bilateral State Grounding and Scoped Passivity for Counterfactual Open-World Deductive Reasoning

Authors: Zeyu Jia, Bo Yao, Zihao Yang

Abstract: Open-World Assumption (OWA) deduction requires distinguishing provable facts (True), refutations (False), and unprovable statements (Unknown) across counterfactual edits and unseen domains. Sequence and latent-recurrent transformers rely on distributional shortcuts, whereas chain-of-thought language models incur high autoregressive latency. We decompose OWA deduction into a clause-factored bilateral grounder ($F$) and a deductive deliberation operator ($B$), forming $B \circ F$ in Typed Edge-Factorized Bilateral Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation (TE-BPHD). Trained solely on three-way query labels without parsers or proof-depth annotations, TE-BPHD (37.85M parameters) maps clauses into a 1,440-dimensional Belnap space and propagates support through passivity-balanced implicit Port-Hamiltonian rule diodes. Across 17,036 locked ProofWriter test queries, TE-BPHD reaches 79.17 +/- 5.48% depth-5 counterfactual consistency (+23.83 to +38.67 percentage points over Seq2Seq T5, Flan-T5, and Recurrent-Depth T5) and 94.95 +/- 1.23% out-of-distribution (OOD) accuracy across 5,270 six-domain queries (92.54 +/- 3.38% macro-mean). On a paired 120-query OOD slice, TE-BPHD attains 93.61 +/- 4.59% versus 79.17% for 3-shot Qwen3-1.7B under a 2,048-token reasoning cap (+14.44 percentage points) at 9.44 versus 14,878.4 ms/query batch-16 NVIDIA L4 throughput. Operator interventions show that symmetric single-rule passivity prevents positive two-cycle modes seen in Gain-Matched Unilateral Horn (+50.00 percentage points counterfactual consistency), whereas unit-interval Grounded Product-T-Norm Logic Tensor Networks (LTN) achieve cycle-safe OOD transfer (94.50 +/- 0.87%) without linear solves, and multi-fact ParaRules prose exposes an upstream grounding trade-off (1.33 +/- 1.76% zero-shot vs. 34.11 +/- 5.01% with five-slot optimal-transport joint grounding).

replace Optimizer-dependent training dynamics converge to the same one-third optimal data scaling

Authors: Hyunseok Lee, Mihir Basil, Yizhou Liu, Jeff Gore

Abstract: Neural scaling, in which loss falls as a power law with training, is central to large language models, and one recent proposal is that a $1/3$ exponent emerges from learning peaked distributions. That account describes SGD, but models in practice are trained with adaptive optimizers. Here we separate two exponents the $1/3$ account does not distinguish: how fast the loss falls with training steps along a single run, and how fast the optimally tuned loss falls with dataset size $D$. We show that the first, a dynamic exponent, is optimizer-specific while the second, an optimal data exponent, converges to $1/3$ across optimizers. In an online teacher-student model we decompose the loss into norm growth (radial) and alignment toward the teacher direction (tangential), each decaying as a power law with dynamic exponents $\alpha_{r}$ and $\alpha_{t}$. Under SGD, both are close to $1/3$, so the data exponent is also $1/3$ across different learning rates. Under Adam the two separate: $\alpha_{r} \simeq 0.48$ but $\alpha_{t} \simeq 0.08$. Since the total loss is minimized when these two parts are balanced, the optimal learning rate is optimizer-dependent: $D$-independent for SGD but falls with $D$ for Adam. Yet tuned to that optimum, the loss returns to $D^{-1/3}$ for both. A stochastic-dynamics analysis explains why: the optimizers can trade decay speed between the two channels, but they all fall on a single dynamic exponent relation, $2\alpha_{r}+ \alpha_{t} = 1$, which fixes the optimal data exponent at $1/3$. Across seven optimizers, including Muon, the measured exponents are consistent with this relation, and the optimal-loss envelopes agree with $D^{-1/3}$ across them. The optimizer sets how fast a model learns per step; tuned optimally, it changes the prefactor but not the rate at which loss falls per sample.

replace Predictive Self-Supervised Learning Provably Identifies Stochastic Signals under Nuisance

Authors: Fabian A. Mikulasch, Friedemann Zenke

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) by predicting in latent space, without generating the input data itself, learns highly abstract, useful representations. Intuitively, this success is often attributed to its ability to discard nuisance information that is irrelevant to prediction. However, this poses a conundrum: both stochastic variation in a prediction-relevant latent signal and true nuisance make observations partly unpredictable; how could they be distinguished? Surprisingly, we prove that common SSL methods can achieve exactly this, by implicitly instantiating a latent-variable model with stochastic dynamics and observation-private nuisance. We trace their ability to recover the stochastic signal to two complementary principles: Predictive mutual information maximization ensures that representations retain the information needed for prediction, while latent distribution matching constrains how this information is encoded, thereby making the retained signal identifiable. We confirm this identifiability result in simulations for Gaussian predictors, which recover the true signal up to an affine transformation even in dynamic, nuisance-laden environments.

replace Characterizing High Bandwidth Flash for LLM Serving

Authors: Zack Yu, Chloe Wong, Coleman Hooper, Minjae Lee, Wonjun Kang, Youngjin Cho, Michael W. Mahoney, Yakun Sophia Shao, Kurt Keutzer, Amir Gholami

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) serving requires substantial memory to store model weights and KV caches. Agentic workloads compound this pressure through repeated interactions over growing contexts, making it important to retain KV cache for reuse. High-bandwidth flash (HBF) offers a way to expand accelerator memory capacity for LLM serving, but its access costs and limited write endurance complicate its use. We evaluate HBF for high-throughput agentic serving across system design and scheduling choices to understand when additional capacity improves serving performance and energy efficiency. We introduce an HBM-HBF-host hierarchical storage system and buffered cache-aware scheduling, and use trace-driven simulations to analyze their effects on performance, energy consumption, and HBF write lifetime. Across the evaluated workloads, the fastest HBF-augmented systems reduce completion time by 21.1-86.9% relative to HBM-only systems. Modeled energy savings reach 62.4%, with benefits depending on the workload and weight placement. Buffered cache-aware scheduling extends estimated HBF write lifetime from 0.99 to 13.63 years in the evaluated configuration. These results demonstrate the importance of coordinating data placement and scheduling in serving LLM with HBF, positioning HBF as an actively utilized lower-level memory tier rather than passive storage.

replace Towards Better Exploration in Sequential Test-Time Scaling

Authors: Joseph Rance, Fabio Pizzati, Juil Sock, Woody Bayliss, Marc G\'orriz Blanch, Philip Torr, Adel Bibi

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves language model reasoning by spending additional compute at inference. However, both classes of existing methods often fail to continue improving over long timescales. Parallel methods repeatedly sample independent answers from the model, scaling poorly on problems the model is unlikely to solve in a single attempt. In contrast, sequential methods build on previous answers to access new ideas, yet so far have not been shown to reach answers beyond those found by parallel scaling. First, we show that sequential scaling often stops improving because it becomes prematurely trapped in an attractor: a set of answers that prevents exploration of different answers once entered. Across 27 combinations of scaling methods, models, and benchmarks, we find that 53.8% of sequential scaling trajectories enter an attractor within four iterations. Second, we show that a simple model-mixing intervention helps escape attractors. This reduces the attractor hit rate by 21.2 percentage points on average, expands solution coverage beyond a compute-matched parallel baseline, and improves accuracy of recursive self-aggregation by at least 2.2 percentage points. Our results motivate refocusing long-horizon test-time scaling from parallel methods to sequential methods that improve previous answers.

replace ArrivalBench: Agent-Generated Data Pipelines Are Correct Once and Wrong Under Time

Authors: Pranay Kothari

Abstract: Benchmarks for agent-generated data work grade a pipeline by running it once against a fixed snapshot. ArrivalBench instead re-executes the pipeline an agent leaves behind under adversarial but replayable delivery schedules (late, duplicated, out-of-order and retried records) and requires its final state to equal a batch recomputation of the complete log. Because the oracle recomputes rather than classifies, a wrong table and a crash are distinct verdicts: a crash is visible to monitoring a team already runs, and a wrong table is not. On 40 tasks we built, our reimplementation of single-execution grading certifies 86-100% of the pipelines eleven models produce; re-executing the same artifacts finds 7.0-79.2% of the certified ones silently wrong. The gap is not produced by the repair loop: within the same model and task, pipelines repaired against the snapshot test fail replay about as often as those that passed it first time. In every model, idempotency hazards fail more often than ordering hazards. Separating a wrong answer from a crash also changes how interventions read: a hazard warning cuts one model's silent failure from 48.2% to 10.5% while raising its crash rate from 9.0% to 37.0%, so all-in failure moves only from 51.0% to 44.0%. All eleven arms were independently re-run, and rates moved by at most 5.9 points.

replace Compact set-valued deep ensembling in multi-class classification

Authors: Kim-Dung Tran, Dang-Man Nguyen, Vu-Linh Nguyen, Xuan-Truong Hoang, S\'ebastien Destercke, Van-Nam Huynh

Abstract: This paper tackles visible challenges in deep ensemble learning, where deep neural networks serve as ensemble members: training and storage burdens, and robustness of cautious (set-valued) predictions targeting multiple utilities, which may involve reward-sensitivity. To mitigate the training and storage burdens, we propose to employ compact ensembles, such as Bayesian Neural Networks and Convolutional Neural Networks with the Monte-Carlo dropout prediction option, to produce probabilistic predictions. For each query instance, these probabilistic predictions are then used to define a representative distribution optimizing some statistical distance. The representative distribution is then employed to define the Bayes-optimal prediction (BOP) of any utility. To address the potential unrobustness of singleton prediction making, we propose a family of set-utilities satisfying some desirable properties and whose set-valued BOPs can be found efficiently. Empirical evidence is then given to illustrate the potential (dis)advantages of the proposed ensemble learning framework.

replace Xaurora: Generative Weather Forecasting with Denoising Stochastic Interpolants from a Foundation Model Prior

Authors: Eliot Walt, Miltiadis Kofinas, Nikolaj M\"ucke, Efstratios Gavves, Dim Coumou

Abstract: Deep learning has revolutionised weather forecasting in recent years, especially through atmospheric foundation models, which offer competitive skill for a fraction of the computational costs of classic physics-based models. However, most existing foundation models are deterministic, limiting the generation of large ensembles for accurate uncertainty quantification, extreme weather risk assessment, and long-range weather forecasting. Furthermore, these models incur a large, often prohibitive, computational overhead to train from scratch. To address these shortcomings, we turn a pretrained deterministic prior model, namely the Aurora foundation model, into a generative ensemble-prediction model. To that end, we introduce a novel generative method, Denoising Stochastic Interpolants, combined with a replay buffer for Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) rollout, enabling probabilistic training of SDE trajectories. Our stochastic foundation model, Xaurora, is finetuned from the small Aurora version, yet it approaches the state-of-the-art on global ensemble metrics and is competitive with the large version of Aurora. Our method is parameter and sample efficient, and generates skilful 15-day forecasts in 7 minutes. Our results demonstrate that deterministic foundation models can be efficiently extended into even stronger stochastic models.

replace The Dichotomy Between Pattern Recognition and Step-by-Step Reasoning

Authors: Amrut Nadgir, Vijay Balasubramanian, Pratik Chaudhari

Abstract: We argue that pattern recognition and step-by-step reasoning are two ends of a spectrum. A large language model (LLM) learns to reason step-by-step when data is structured such that the next token depends on a small amount of preceding context. Inference in LLMs resembles pattern recognition when the next token depends on a large amount of preceding context. If the next token depends on only the $c$ most recent tokens, reasoning traces are paths on a de Bruijn graph whose nodes are $c$-length contexts and edges are next-token transitions between contexts. The set of reasoning traces of a task forms a directed acyclic subgraph of the de Bruijn graph. An LLM that has learned all edges of this subgraph can compose them to solve longer, unseen tasks, i.e., it reasons step-by-step. We prove that the number of edges is vanishingly small compared to the number of reasoning traces. We also prove and show empirically that the number of training samples a transformer needs is a linear in the number of edges, so learning to reason step-by-step is sample efficient. We can induce de Bruijn structure in any task by maintaining a ``state'' that makes future reasoning independent of the past. The frequency of states in the reasoning trace determines $c$. We show, by fine-tuning Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct to solve equations and answer questions about stories, that frequent states (small $c$) result in higher accuracy but greater fragility to perturbations at test time. LLMs trained with a large $c$ are only as good as models that perform pattern recognition without reasoning. A moderate density of states balances accuracy and robustness. Real-world data has de Bruijn structure: Qwen3-14B and Qwen3-32B retain over 75\% of their accuracy on GSM8K, MATH-500 and GPQA-Diamond when attention is restricted to a sliding window less than 15\% as long as the full reasoning trace.

replace Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization: Unified Learning from Offline Demonstrations and On- and Off-Policy Rollouts via a Shared Regression Target

Authors: Du Cheng, Junyu Lu, Jingfan Zhang, Haoxuan Jia, Shichao Weng

Abstract: Reasoning post-training draws on offline demonstrations and policy-generated rollouts, yet conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) separates their objectives and limits continued reuse of collected responses. We propose \textbf{Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization} (CBPO), which makes these sources compatible with one regression loss through a fixed-reference target. Because the generating policy does not enter this target, positive and negative offline completions, reference responses, and current and historical rollouts can share a common reward-labeled record format and replay buffer. As new responses enter the buffer, optimization retains the same regression objective without coordinating separate SFT and RL objectives or requiring stored behavior-policy probabilities, importance weighting, or a learned critic. Restricting the available sources gives offline, on-policy, and off-policy variants, while CBPO-Unified combines them. Across two model scales and four mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CBPO delivers leading \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} performance across benchmarks and data regimes. On Qwen3-1.7B, CBPO-Unified improves benchmark-averaged \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} over SFT DAPO by 2.33 and 4.44 percentage points, respectively. In the replay ablation, retaining historical rollouts improves these metrics by 1.87 and 3.72 percentage points over the no-history configuration.

replace Mixing Regularization for Average-Reward $Q$-Learning and $Q$-Planning

Authors: Julien Roger, Sam Bodmer, Jian Qian, Benjamin Riviere

Abstract: The average-reward setting for reinforcement learning is challenging because, without discounting, the span-contraction of the Bellman operator depends on the mixing properties of the underlying Markov dynamics. Whereas existing work typically assumes contraction, for instance through environment mixing, we study regularization toward a given reward-agnostic, $m$-step mixing policy to control the mixing properties of the learned policy. Our central mixing lemma quantifies how the reference policy's mixing properties transfer to the resulting learned policies. In particular, a single regularizer-dependent quantity simultaneously controls the contraction of the regularized Bellman operator and the exploration of the resulting policies. A second regularizer-dependent quantity controls the bias introduced by regularization, revealing a trade-off between convergence speed and approximation accuracy. Building on this result, we establish finite-time guarantees for regularized $Q$-iteration, synchronous and single-trajectory $Q$-learning, and $Q$-planning. Under one-step mixing, our bounds for approximating the regularized fixed point match the classical model-query bound for relative value iteration and, up to logarithmic factors, an existing sample-complexity bound for synchronous $Q$-learning. We also establish finite-time guarantees under multi-step mixing. For $Q$-planning, we characterize how the planning horizon trades off boundary bias against sample complexity. Finally, we present numerical studies validating the analysis: (i) knowledge of a strong mixing policy improves convergence and exploration, with larger benefits under more severe bottlenecks; and (ii) the observed rankings of $\alpha$-divergence regularizers by bias and convergence speed are consistent with our theoretical analysis.

replace Refined Topology-Pass Scheduling: Damped Trust-Region Compensation for Dynamic k-NN Graphs in SE(3)-Equivariant Structure Prediction

Authors: Jiankai Huang, Hanwen Shen

Abstract: Equivariant structure-prediction pipelines alternate coordinate updates with the construction of a k-nearest-neighbour (kNN) graph whose membership is a discrete, piecewise-constant function of the coordinates. Rebuilding that graph at every block boundary (PB) is the expensive part of an epoch, whereas reusing one frozen graph (F) is fast but leaves a topology gap. We study a two-pass design in which the cheap pass records a small amount of state (margins, kNN radii, measured per-boundary displacements) and a second pass compensates conservatively. We formulate the choice of the graph-construction basis as a damped fixed-point iteration whose unique fixed point is exactly PB, and derive (i) exact non-flip certificates with the two-sided constant $m_i/4$ and one-sided constant $m_i/2$, (ii) the minimum-variance (BLUE) replay gain $\gamma^*$ and the resulting feasibility crossover, (iii) an $\ell_\infty$ trust-region projection that preserves convergence, and (iv) an analytic near-tie spacing law $E[m] \approx r_k/(3k)$ that matches measurements to 1\%. Three compensation levels are implemented in C++ (L1: frozen set with live geometry; L2: margin-gated local repair; L3: damped, projected basis) and validated under a matched-conditions protocol (identical seed, MSA depth and trajectory family, only the topology mode differs). L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly ($D = 0.0000$ at every boundary), L1 stays within 0.6% and L3 interpolates monotonically: L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly when the trust region is removed, and L3 interpolates between the frozen and the PB topology. The recorded trajectory itself yields a measured $1.5\times$ Pass-1 and $1.76\times$ epoch speed-up with no measurable loss gap. An open-source implementation of the runtime and of all the experiments reported here is available at https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

URLs: https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

replace Forecasting Patient Trajectories from the Electronic Health Records with Action-Conditioned World Models

Authors: Simon A. Lee, Jiahang Hank Sha, Soomin Chung, Kai Akamatsu, Eran Halperin, Jeffrey N. Chiang

Abstract: We introduce a predictive world model for electronic health records (EHR) that represents patient histories as action conditioned latent trajectories. Rather than modeling clinical events solely through autoregressive token prediction, our framework explicitly separates patient states from observed clinical actions and learns their evolution through a state and action conditioned transition operator. At its core is a state dependent linearization predictor that propagates local changes in latent patient state, enabling time resolved prediction, multi step rollout, and action conditioned simulation. Empirically, we show that generated latent trajectories preserve downstream predictive signal, temporal structure, and subgroup level statistical relationships present in encoder derived representations. We further decode free running rollouts into the original clinical feature space and evaluate future clinical measurements against observed values using task matched forecasting baselines, demonstrating that the learned dynamics retain predictive information beyond latent space agreement. Across binary classification and time to event tasks, EHR WM achieves competitive, though task dependent, performance relative to substantially larger EHR foundation models while using a 30x smaller parameter footprint. Finally, perturbing observed action sequences produces corresponding changes in generated trajectories, demonstrating the action sensitivity of the learned dynamics. Together, these results support world modeling as a complementary framework for learning explicit, time indexed representations of patient evolution from longitudinal EHR.

replace REACT: Representational Early Analysis and Control of Chain-of-Thought Trajectories

Authors: Hanwen Zhang, Enyi Jiang, Ruike Zhu, Qinan Yu, Sanmi Koyejo

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) generation offers an opportunity to intervene before a reasoning model completes its answer. How does the safety effect of a single activation edit vary across early reasoning states, and what factors govern that variation? We study this question with REACT, a conditional activation intervention that learns a safety direction from paired safe and unsafe trajectories and applies it once to an early CoT hidden state. The model weights remain frozen, and all subsequent tokens are generated without further edits. To study timing, we train directions separately at each of the first five CoT positions and evaluate ungated edits with full post-edit regeneration. Across three DeepSeek-R1 distilled models, site-specific edits concentrate safety gains in the opening CoT tokens. Norm-matched controls on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (8B) disentangle two factors behind this curve: holding an effective direction and edit budget fixed still yields much larger early ($t_1$-$t_5$) than later ($t_6$-$t_8$) gains, whereas the near-zero site-specific $t_3$ gain reflects higher optimisation variance despite rising risk-probe AUROC. Forced prefixes shift the high-gain region with the opening context, and a four-arm intervention shows that ~98% of the net gain at $t_2$ propagates through the changed next token. On the same 8B model, REACT raises the rate of fully safe CoT-answer outputs on StrongREJECT-273 from 29.06% to 82.05%, while keeping benign refusal and usefulness near baseline. An anonymized implementation is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/REACT_official2-333B.

replace Physiological Trajectories and Observation Patterns in ICU Mortality Prediction: Transportability Under Changing Measurement Conditions

Authors: Benjamin T. Pike

Abstract: Objective: To examine how changes in clinical measurement practices affect the transportability of predictive information from physiological trajectories and observation patterns in longitudinal ICU records. Methods: In-hospital mortality was predicted using measurements from the first 48 hours of ICU admission in MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU. replace Generative Forecasting from Coarse Observations: Does Cascading Help?

Authors: Alexandra Spitzer, Giovanni Abati, Lorenzo Baldassari, Till Muser, Cheng Shi, Ivan Dokmani\'c

Abstract: We study probabilistic forecasting of dynamical systems such as fluids and waves from coarse observations. Since the unseen fine scales influence the dynamics, the same coarse initial state can evolve into a whole distribution of futures. Generative forecasters may produce visually plausible samples, but it is hard to tell whether they sample the right conditional distribution, since trajectory data contain a single future for each initial state. We address this problem by generating reference data in a hierarchical way: for each coarse initial state, we generate many different fine-scale components and numerically evolve the full states. Using these conditional ensembles, we show that standard diffusion models and stochastic interpolants can make substantial distributional errors when trained over the full forecast horizon with finite data and model capacity, despite being exact in principle. We then show that cascading shorter forecasts greatly reduces these errors for the turbulent flows and waves we study, but we also find and characterize cases in which it hurts. Since the coarse process is not Markov, cascading discards information about the unresolved fine scales even with exact transitions. To understand this, we propose a solvable analytic model which shows that the optimal cascade depth decreases as the coupling between the unresolved and observed scales gets stronger, and increases as the dependence of the forecast on the observation becomes more oscillatory. Consistent with our experiments, cascading is detrimental when the direct forecast is "easy" to learn, for example Gaussian as in the passive scalar case. We release our datasets as a first benchmark for probabilistic forecasting with ground-truth conditional ensembles.

replace Best-of-Depth: Learning Exploratory Dynamics in Looped Transformers

Authors: Shota Takashiro, Takeshi Kojima, Masanori Koyama, Kohei Hayashi, Yusuke Iwasawa

Abstract: Looped Transformers extend inference-time computation through repeated latent-state updates, but final-depth training does not directly encourage useful intermediate alternatives. We introduce Best-of-Depth (BoD), which trains a recurrent trajectory as a candidate set by minimizing prediction loss across depths. Different valid targets can be matched at different depths without architectural changes or depth-specific labels. An exact-match analysis connects this objective to solution coverage. On N-Queens completion and graph coloring, BoD improves coverage over final-depth and intermediate-supervision objectives. N-Queens coverage reaches 91.03%, versus 45.75% for final-depth training. Further experiments show discovery beyond the training depth and recovery of solutions withheld from teacher pools on familiar inputs. Fine-tuning a pretrained looped language model with BoD yields higher pooled code-generation success than final-depth training when programs are generated separately at evaluated fixed depths. These results support training recurrent computation for solution discovery along its trajectory.

replace The Alignment Tax Is Graded: Task-Selective Capability Loss Under Length-Normalized DPO with SFT Retention

Authors: Ye Yuan

Abstract: Setting. This is an empirical characterization study; we propose no new method and run no comparisons against alternative objectives. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B with sparse LoRA and length-normalized DPO plus an SFT retention term (the RPO objective) on structured financial-filing tasks, and track downstream capability as the implicit reward margin grows to $\sim 7$ $\beta$-scaled units (nf4 training; $\sim 3$ under fp16 measurement on the unquantized base). The margin is accounted for almost entirely ($\geq 96\%$) by suppression of rejected completions, with chosen reward approximately flat, consistent with the retention term anchoring it; we do not attribute the behavioral damage to any single gradient channel. Length-normalized rewards with SFT retention, sparse LoRA on 8/32 layers, one 4B model, easily-separable synthetic preferences, three structured tasks. A no-retention control and a measured format-alignment metric are left to future work.

replace Context--Answer Metamodels: Forecasting LLM Activations Before the First Answer Token

Authors: Tommy He, Thomas Jiralerspong

Abstract: Under stochastic decoding, a single context can elicit many different answers from a language model. Characterizing this variation typically requires many expensive autoregressive rollouts, each revealing only one realization. We ask whether the activations produced while reading the context can directly forecast the model's rollout activations. We train context--answer metamodels that map a context activation sequence to point or distribution forecasts of mean-pooled answer activations. We find that (i) the four metamodel families we study forecast mean-pooled answer activations with high fidelity, and the distribution metamodels also capture rollout variability; (ii) performance improves as the training set grows to 500,000 prompts, and the metamodels generalize zero-shot to out-of-domain datasets; (iii) their forecasts retain behavior-predictive signal; and (iv) context activations from Qwen2.5 predict mean-pooled answer activations from Qwen3.5 and Gemma 4, suggesting that activation forecasts can transfer across models. Together, these results suggest that context activation traces can support scalable forecasts of behaviors in LLM answers before the first answer token is generated.

replace Attention at Absolute Zero

Authors: Mostafa 'Neo' Mohsenvand

Abstract: Attention scores candidate supports of an answer, which we call witnesses, and reads the scores at a temperature. We build a higher-order attention layer whose state lives on pairs of items: for a pair (a, b), each third item k is a witness judged through its legs (a, k) and (k, b). It reads witnesses at zero temperature, keeping the best and the number tied with it, passes on only bits comparing such counts, rebuilds its pair state from the input and these bits at every loop, and is trained from answers alone. Each choice removes one way soft attention fails at size: dispersed weight, unresolved gaps between learned scores, and a drifting soft state. In maximum retrieval it is right on at least 99.95% of sets 1,000 times larger than in training, on all ten seeds; trained with a soft count, the same network falls to 42% at 4,096 items. On CLRS-30 without hints, a general form of the layer (half its channels read at zero temperature, numbers embedded, state carried) beats the best published model, which uses hints on some tasks; the full cold layer is exact on every test input of 9 of the 12 tasks whose answers depend on numbers only through comparisons. From yes-or-no answers it decides 2-SAT and two-colouring exactly at 500 variables and, as our theory predicts, not parity equations. Beside a frozen 7B language model it chains family relations exactly at 1,000 statements after training on 16, where the model alone falls to 44%; finite checks on the weights prove every block that learned correct at every size, on acyclic contexts and given what the model reads. On CLUTRR it is right on every story of five to ten relations given the graphs, learning the 62 rules exactly; from text, with the model frozen, no hand-written rule and only a small read-in trained, it lifts an open 7B model from 33% to 65%, above every system we found without hand-written rules except frontier reasoning models.

replace When Does Causality Help in Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection?

Authors: Long Zhang, Wei Huang, Aishui Rao, Jian Hu, Binxiang Wu, Jie Li, Chen Gong, Linxi Feng

Abstract: Causality-aware scoring is common in multivariate time-series anomaly detection, yet the literature rarely states when it helps. Holding one causality-guided autoencoder (DeCauAE) fixed, we vary graph quality, anomaly type and protocol against ground truth. Graph quality fixes the payoff: on a twenty-variable DAG the gain over a graph-free autoencoder decays as the graph is corrupted. Anomaly type fixes the target: the structural factor lifts window F1 from 0.800 to 0.976 where anomalies break conditional structure, and its advantage vanishes below a break severity s 0.3. replace LazyWeights: Decode-on-Demand Edge LLMs

Authors: Arnab Sanyal, Gourav Datta, Sandeep P. Chinchali

Abstract: Memory determines which large language models (LLMs) an edge device can run and how fast it runs them: capacity limits a 4 GB Jetson Nano, and bandwidth limits an 8 GB Jetson Orin Nano Super. Deployed weight formats (GPTQ, AWQ, and the GGUF K- and I-quants) spend the same number of bits on every integer code of a matrix, although the codes are far from uniform. We present LazyWeights, which keeps these formats, tunes GPTQ so the codes compress, Huffman-codes them losslessly in interleaved lanes, and decodes them on demand in a small unit between DRAM and L2, so GPU kernels run unchanged and DRAM moves only coded bytes. At 1% and 3.5% perplexity budgets, coded Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B and SmolLM-1.7B are 5.7-31.4% smaller than the best GGUF format. A bit-exact 0.21 mm^2 ASIC decoder unit (1 GHz, 7 nm) cuts Orin latency and energy per token by up to 36% and 38% (54% and 44% with prompt-lookup speculation). On the Nano, LazyWeights needs 58-64% less time and 53-57% less energy per token than the GGUF runtime on the CPU, and runs a streamed Mistral-7B 6.6x faster at 3.89 bits per weight. A Huffman decoder beats an rANS decoder in rate and area at equal throughput, and the unit runs bit-exact on a Zynq UltraScale+ FPGA (AMD ZCU102).

replace Federated Physics-Informed Thermal Field Prediction Across Unseen Geometries in Distributed Additive Manufacturing

Authors: Amirul Islam Saimon, Zhenyu James Kong

Abstract: In additive manufacturing (AM), each change in part geometry requires a new thermal simulation, which makes design evaluation over many candidates costly. Geometry-aware models reduce this cost by predicting thermal fields on unseen parts with a single learned model, but they assume that diverse geometries are available centrally. This assumption is restrictive when each manufacturing site holds only a limited set of proprietary part designs that it cannot share. We propose Fed-PIGANO, a federated framework that trains physics-informed geometry-aware neural operator (PI-GANO) models at individual sites and aggregates their geometry encoders and prediction networks without sharing part geometries. Over seven replications, Fed-PIGANO achieves mean relative L2 errors of 1.83% to 2.04% across all 12 scenario-site cases, compared with up to 20.46% for local-only training, and consistently yields lower PDE residuals. Its prediction accuracy is comparable to that of a centralized model trained on the pooled geometries of all sites. These results demonstrate that federated learning lets manufacturing sites benefit from each other's geometries without sharing them, enabling rapid thermal prediction on new part designs for tasks such as shape optimization, inverse design, and uncertainty quantification.

Authors: Mykhailo Buleshnyi, Zsolt Zombori, Michael Benedikt

Abstract: We introduce NeSySy, a synthetic generator for neurosymbolic learning tasks that jointly produces logical constraints and consistent datasets. Unlike existing benchmarks, NeSySy provides fine-grained control over key properties such as constraint structure, tightness, input dependence, and data generation and alignment strategies. Satisfiability checkers (CNFgen and Z3) generate and certify constraints. Constrained optimizers (RC2 for MaxSAT, Z3 for OMT) compute optimal repairs that guarantee every generated sample is consistent.

Abstract: Generative modeling in science is often data-limited, where experiments are expensive. However, each experiment may yield several coupled measurements of the same physical entity. Authors: Kushagra Bainsla

Abstract: In the global attention layers of Gemma 4 31B, the value is the normalized key projection, and the key is the value scaled by a fixed weight, with rotary position encoding on one quarter of its dimensions. replace Variance-Optimal Counterfactual Policy Design for Sample-Efficient Online A/B Testing

Authors: Shashank Gupta, Subhadeep Maji

Abstract: Online A/B testing is the standard approach for evaluating changes to user-facing systems, such as a new recommendation or ranking policy, before deployment. While the difference-in-means estimator of a standard A/B test is unbiased, it often suffers from high variance. When the treatment and production policies choose the same actions with similar probabilities, the resulting observations can add noise while providing little information about the difference between the policies. Consequently, A/B tests can require large amounts of traffic and long durations to detect small treatment effects. Existing variance-reduction methods mainly improve the estimator after the data-collection scheme has been fixed, and recent overlap-aware designs only optimize a single traffic split between the two policies; thereby, they cannot direct traffic to the actions on which the policies disagree. In this paper, we jointly optimize how actions are sampled and how the resulting observations are used to estimate the treatment effect. Our contributions are two-fold. First, building on pairwise off-policy evaluation, we derive the variance-optimal action-level logging policy for estimating the average treatment effect between two policies, which has a closed-form solution for any fixed control variate. Second, we use historical logs to jointly optimize the logging policy and a control variate to further reduce variance. Our experimental results in simulated policy A/B tests show that our designs estimate the average treatment effect more accurately than existing variance reduction methods on every evaluated setting, while generally improving statistical power and reducing how often the worse policy is selected. As a result, our designs enable practitioners to reach reliable deployment decisions with considerably less online traffic.

Authors: Anant Mathur, Sarat Moka, Zdravko Botev

Abstract: In addition to recent developments in computing speed and memory, methodological advances have contributed to significant gains in the performance of stochastic simulation.
