In side-by-side human evaluation, Kandinsky 6.0 Video Pro clearly outperforms its predecessor, Kandinsky 5.0 Video Pro, and remains competitive with leading audio-video generation models, outperforming LTX 2.5 and Seedance 2.0 in speech quality. To accelerate open research and deployment in multimedia generation, we release the code, model checkpoints, and diffusers integration under the MIT license.

replace-cross ResearchFlow-Eval: Tracing the Execution Gap in End-to-End Multi-Claim Autoresearch

Authors: Peng Jiang, Dongyang Li, Quanying Liu, Xiaoxuan Jia

Abstract: Autoresearch systems increasingly undertake entire research projects. In practice, scientific research requires systems to pursue multiple interdependent claims through a continuous, multi-stage workflow and revise experiments as evidence accumulates. Existing evaluations of autoresearch systems, however, typically score isolated tasks or final artifacts, leaving these process-level capabilities largely unmeasured. We therefore introduce ResearchFlow-Eval, a fine-grained framework for assessing continuous scientific execution and iteration at both the stage and claim levels. Each evaluation is grounded in a published anchor paper, from which we derive stage-specific rubrics that decompose each research stage into dimensions and separately scored rubric items. Using these rubrics, we score each research stage and examine experimental execution in detail, focusing on multi-claim completion and revisions across attempts. We apply the evaluation framework to general-purpose agent harnesses, skill-augmented harnesses, and systems with research-specific orchestration. Averaged over two runs per system, each scored by two LLM judges, experimental execution lags behind design and planning for every evaluated system. Within execution, systems struggle to produce high-quality results and meet claim requirements, and repeated attempts rarely yield measurable improvements. Moreover, neither skill augmentation nor research-specific orchestration reliably yields higher scores. ResearchFlow-Eval thus extends autoresearch evaluation beyond isolated, single-objective runs to continuous workflows in which systems pursue multiple claims and refine their work across iterations.

replace-cross EgoHand: Monocular 3D Hand Reconstruction from Egocentric Video

Authors: Chao-Yeh Chen, Tiffany Chen, Lin Duan, Tianyi Hu, Yu-Hsiang Huang, Umar Iqbal, Jiefeng Li, Edy Lim, Yanchen Liu, Yihan Pang, Hang Qiu, Jun Saito, Shao Su, Qingsi Wang, Xiufeng Xie

Abstract: We introduce EgoHand, a monocular 3D hand reconstruction model that achieves state-of-the-art performance on egocentric video. Central to our approach is the curation of a high-quality training corpus of 14 million images, combining synthetic data, in-the-wild video, and multi-camera motion capture to balance scene diversity and supervision quality. The model adopts SOMA-Hand as its parametric representation and processes 2D hand crops with a transformer encoder, incorporating camera cues and an auxiliary 2D branch to improve geometric consistency and image alignment. Zero-shot evaluations on HOT3D, ARCTIC, and H2O demonstrate strong out-of-domain generalization, with lower joint-position, root-translation, and acceleration errors than competing baselines at an inference latency of approximately 9.01 ms. replace-cross Probing the limits of materials design via a comprehensive crystal database of 14.6 million structures with 140 properties

Authors: Zhilong Song, Zijun Pang, Lixue Cheng

Abstract: Forward screening and inverse design drive materials discovery, yet their limits remain unquantified because records of known crystal structures and properties are fragmented across databases. Consequently, forward screening leaves known crystals underexplored for new functions, whereas generative and autonomous discovery has repeatedly reported known phases or close variants as new materials. Here we match structures across 55 databases to assemble MateriaViva, a unified database of 14.6 million distinct crystals and over one billion property values. To provide the missing properties of every structure, a task we term property completion, we develop an equivariant Crystal Graph Transformer Network with state-of-the-art accuracy on nine benchmark tasks and apply 140 property-prediction models across the database. These predictions reveal the underestimated potential of forward screening, identifying tens of thousands of high-performance candidates among previously synthesized or computationally studied materials that lack recorded target-property values. Conversely, comparison with MateriaViva shows that the novelty of inverse design has been overestimated, as 55-100% of inverse-designed structures are not new. Under equal density functional theory verification budgets for bulk-modulus design, inverse design yields a higher fraction of qualifying candidates, whereas forward screening reaches the higher bulk modulus. MateriaViva thus provides a comprehensive reference for probing the limits of both paradigms, revealing unrecognized performance in known crystals and directing inverse design beyond reported structures.

replace-cross How many sorted projections determine any n points in k dimensions?

Authors: Christopher Gorham Lester, Raymond Hau S{\o}rensen

Abstract: Let $d_1,\dots,d_m$ be directions in $\mathbb{R}^k$, where $k\ge 2$. A multiset $X$ of $n$ points of $\mathbb{R}^k$ projects to the $m$ multisets of numbers $\{\{x\cdot d_j : x\in X\}\}$, or equivalently to the $m$ sorted lists of these numbers. How many directions are needed for these projections to determine every multiset of $n$ points? Two simple bounds are known in every dimension: more than $(k-1)\log_2 n$ directions are always needed, and $(k-1)n+1$ directions in general position always suffice. In the plane much more is known: Matousek, Privetivy and Skovron showed that almost every choice of $O(\log n \log\log n)$ directions suffices. Whether anything similar holds in higher dimensions was left open by Dym, Wellershoff, Tsoukanis, Levy and Balan. We observe that two lemmas of Hair and Sahai, proved for list recovery of random linear codes, answer that question, and by tightening their argument we show that in every dimension almost every choice of $(4k-2)\ln(2n)\ln(k\log_2 2n)+k+1$ directions suffices. For $k\le 10$ this beats the linear bound once $n$ is larger than about 100: for example, 144 directions suffice for multisets of 1000 points in the plane, and an exact computation lowers this to 133. Letting some of the random blocks in their argument fail removes a factor $\log k$: almost every choice of $O(k\log n\log\log n)$ directions suffices, uniformly in $k$ and $n$. We also show that $2k-1$ directions never determine three points, so that the logarithmic lower bound is not tight; that every $m$ directions in the plane fail on some pair of multisets of size $O(1.65^m)$, improving the bound $O(1.80^m)$ of Matousek, Privetivy and Skovron; and that for each $k$, $m$ and $n$, either almost every choice of $m$ directions works or almost none does.

replace-cross ConDR: Conservative Reachability through Neural Hamilton-Jacobi Subsolutions

Authors: Kendra Givens, Taylor T. Johnson, Bryan C. Ward, Thomas Beckers

Abstract: Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) Reachability identifies the unsafe states of a dynamical system from which it can be forced into failure under worst-case disturbance. However, classical grid-based solvers scale exponentially with state dimension. DeepReach improves scalability by replacing the grid with a neural value function trained on the HJ-Isaacs (HJI) variational inequality (VI), but penalizes both sides of the residual equally. This allows the learned value to lie above the true one and unsafe states to be misclassified as safe. We propose Conservative DeepReach (ConDR) which aims to learn a subsolution of the HJI VI such that the learned unsafe set contains the true one. ConDR enforces the obstacle branch and terminal condition by construction, pushes the HJI residual to a safety margin $\epsilon$ below zero with a one-sided loss, and prevents the degenerate solution of classifying everything as unsafe. We prove that the exact solution of the margin-shifted VI that ConDR trains toward is a subsolution of the original VI so its unsafe set contains the true one and its value lies within $\epsilon\tau$ of the true value function. We compare against DeepReach and MADR in the established systems of Air3D, Air6D, a 9D multi-vehicle system, and a 13D quadrotor. ConDR reports the lowest rate of unsafe state misclassified and the lowest closed-loop collision rates with at most a small increase in intervention. On Air3D and Air6D, ConDR tolerates model mismatch that scales with $\epsilon$. At matched false-negative rates, ConDR's unsafe sets are smaller than the baselines showing the safety is not obtained by trivial over-approximation.

replace-cross On Agentic Manipulation Policies

Authors: Chen Chen, Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr \v{S}vec, David J. Yoon, Matthias M\"uller, Vladlen Koltun

Abstract: Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. See https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/ for code and videos.

URLs: https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/

replace-cross Prediction with Foundation Models under Deployment Budgets

Authors: Zhaohui Xu, Xiaofei Xu, Doudou Zhou

Abstract: Foundation models can supply predictions for a downstream task that draw on knowledge from extensive pretraining, but they differ in task-specific accuracy and query cost. We develop a framework that improves prediction for the task under a budget on the average query cost per input, using training data to learn which models to query and how to combine their outputs with features available without querying. The first method queries the same models for every input; the second lets them vary across inputs and uses Q-learning to decide which model to query next and when to stop. For the first method, we show that several individually inaccurate outputs can jointly outperform the best single one. It attains the minimax rate when the regression function depends on the outputs only through models within the budget. Otherwise, any method within the budget can be forced to a slower rate, which the first method attains. For the second method, we show when varying the queried models across inputs strictly improves on querying the same models for every input, and bound its prediction risk and average cost in finite samples. In simulations and a multimodal laptop price application, both methods outperform existing methods.

replace-cross Classical Energies Learn to Reason 1,000x Faster than Looped Models

Authors: Benjamin Hoover, Hugh Blayney, James C. R. Whittington

Abstract: Looped models have emerged as a prominent paradigm for reasoning, with successes on simple reasoning puzzles such as Sudoku-Extreme and Maze-Unique. However, these models can be unstable, slow to train, and use many parameters and FLOPs to solve simple puzzles. Descent on an energy not only mitigates these issues but also offers advantages: built-in early stopping, compute that adapts to problem difficulty, fast training without differentiating through a solver, and interpretable weights that learn the constraints of the puzzle. Here we demonstrate this with a learned version of Hopfield & Tank's energy from the 80s, which we term CRAM, solves 99.996% of Sudoku-Extreme and 100% of Maze-Unique test puzzles after 42 ms and 132 ms of training on one GPU, at least 1,000x faster than looped models on these tasks. CRAM also matches baseline looped models at their highest accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme using at least 282x fewer inference FLOPs. Our work suggests two things. First, that energies are an extremely promising direction for training reasoning from data. Second, a caution against using trivially solvable benchmarks like Sudoku and Maze to test the scalability of looped reasoning techniques.

replace-cross Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment

Authors: Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI), Soran Parsa (University of Huddersfield)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under four years the field has grown from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. VLA research joins robotics, computer vision and language modelling, so this survey is written for researchers and practitioners from each of these fields. It reviews 89 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. Seven design axes follow a VLA's life cycle: model design (inputs and memory, architecture, action output, intermediate predictions), training (pretraining data, post-training) and deployment (real-time execution). We compare 22 models on these axes, with their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge. (i) Training data has broadened beyond teleoperated robot data (about 10,000 hours for $\pi_0$) to community-collected episodes and egocentric human video (20,000 hours for GR00T N1.7). (ii) Model sizes have split into two groups: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters; a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs. (iii) Generalist VLAs handling both manipulation and navigation are emerging, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions, and review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail after small scene changes. A practitioner's guide gives a default for each design decision, and a glossary supports readers new to the field.

replace-cross Controllable Chains of Thought Can Reveal Less, Even Without Evasion Training

Authors: Zheng-Xin Yong, Benjamin Arnav, Miles Wang, Tomek Korbak, Olivia Watkins

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising tool for detecting hidden goals, off-task behavior, and other failures in AI agents. One risk is that models could deliberately shape their reasoning to make it opaque to monitors. We stress-test this risk through persona-control training: supervised fine-tuning on mathematical reasoning traces rewritten in the voices of diverse personas. This induces a broader ability to control CoT, even though training contains no monitor-evasion examples. In covert side-task evaluations, traces in which a gpt-oss-20b model organism completes the hidden side task contain less monitorable evidence than the base model's. Averaged across evaluations and CoT lengths, weighted by side-task-success counts, the CoT monitorability metric g-mean$^2$ falls from 0.94 for the base model to 0.68 for the model organism without a hiding instruction, and to 0.22 when explicitly instructed to hide. In intervention evaluations, explicit hiding often drives monitorability near zero. The effect extends to a Kimi-K2.6 model organism in agentic environments, though hidden objectives requiring extended reasoning or longer trajectories remain more monitorable than simple single-step side tasks. We further discover that targeted transparency prompts can substantially recover monitorability, sometimes surpassing the base model. These model organisms provide a testbed for studying monitorability failures and potential mitigations. Our code can be found on https://github.com/yongzx/cot-control-model-organism.

replace-cross Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.

replace-cross Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).
